You have the deepest harai capability and the strongest starting kami relationships, but you are bound by the heaviest obligations. When the shrine needs you, you come. When the matsuri calls, you answer. Your power is service, and service is never free.

Miko (Shrine Maiden/Medium): The oracle. You specialize in kagura dance, spirit communication, divination, and trance. You see what others cannot — yokai invisible to ordinary perception, kami in places where no one expected them, the spiritual residue of events that happened centuries ago. But trance makes you vulnerable. When you open yourself to the spirit world, the spirit world can come in uninvited.

Bushi (Warrior-Protector): The defender. You are trained in martial arts alongside spiritual discipline. You are the physically strongest Archetype, the one who stands between the community and what threatens it. But every act of violence accumulates kegare, and a Bushi who fights without purifying becomes the very pollution they were trying to protect against. Your path is the razor's edge — the sword drawn in service, cleaned immediately after.

Onmyōji (Diviner/Exorcist): The specialist in liminal spaces. You work with the boundary between the manifest world and the spirit world — identifying yokai, pacifying restless dead, navigating Yomi, reading the signs of spiritual disturbance. You are the detective, the diagnostician, the one who figures out what went wrong and why. Your tools are divination, ritual precision, and an understanding of cosmological forces that others find abstract but you find practical.

Tabibito (Wandering Pilgrim): The traveler. You are unbound by a single shrine or clan. Where others have deep roots, you have wide roads. You accumulate breadth — relationships with kami across many regions, knowledge of yokai in many habitats, experience of shrines and matsuri from one end of GU Japan to the other. Your starting kami relationships are the weakest, but your eventual network will be the widest. You see connections that rooted characters miss.

## STEP TWO: CHOOSE YOUR CLAN AND UJIGAMI

Your clan is your family — the community you were born into, the land you come from, the shrine where your umbilical cord was buried. Your clan determines your starting region on the map of GU Japan and your initial kami relationships.

Your ujigami is your clan's guardian kami — the tutelary spirit that has watched over your family for generations. Your relationship with your ujigami is the oldest kami relationship on your character sheet. It was established before you were born. It carries expectations and obligations alongside its blessings. The Kannushi will help you determine your clan and ujigami based on the region and the story being told.

## STEP THREE: ASSIGN YOUR VIRTUES

Place the four scores — +2, +1, +1, +0 — on your four Virtues: Nintai, Satori, Yuuki, Makoto.

Think about who your Aruji is. Think about what comes naturally to them and what they struggle with. A Miko who sees spirits easily might have +2 Satori and +0 Yuuki — great perception, but the things she sees terrify her. A Bushi who charges into danger without hesitation might have +2 Yuuki and +0 Nintai — all courage, no patience. An Onmyōji who keeps every promise to every spirit might have +2 Makoto and +0 Satori — deeply sincere, but sometimes missing what is hidden in plain sight.

Your +0 Virtue is not a weakness. It is a promise. It is the Virtue the game will develop, the Lesson the kami will keep presenting, the growing edge where your most meaningful satori will eventually occur.

## STEP FOUR: SET UP YOUR KEGARE TRACK

Draw ten empty boxes. You begin clean. Enjoy it while it lasts.

## STEP FIVE: SET UP YOUR KAMI RELATIONSHIP TRACKER

Write down your ujigami. Note that you have a starting relationship — you were raised under this kami's care. The depth and quality of that starting relationship depends on your Archetype (a Kannushi starts with a deeper shrine relationship than a Tabibito) and your backstory (which you and the Kannushi will develop together).

As the game progresses, you will add new kami to this tracker — every shrine you visit, every kami you encounter, every relationship you build or damage. This tracker is the story of your spiritual life, written in the names of the beings who have known you.

## STEP SIX: CHOOSE YOUR STARTING GIFTS

Based on your Archetype, you begin with specific tools, knowledge, and connections. The Kannushi will provide these during character creation. A Kannushi character might begin with a specific shrine, ritual implements, and knowledge of basic norito prayers. A Bushi might begin with a weapon, martial training, and a protective duty. A Tabibito might begin with a travel kit, a map, and letters of introduction to distant shrines.

These starting gifts are not "equipment" in the traditional RPG sense. They are relationships — with objects, with places, with communities. In a world where everything is alive, even your walking staff has a story to tell.

## ORACLE CARDS IN THE LIVING WORLD

The oracle card system from the core Temple of Gu game integrates seamlessly into Shinto No Kami. Oracle cards are optional — the game plays beautifully with dice alone — but for groups that want to deepen their experience, oracle cards transform the table into a genuine divinatory practice.

The three types of oracle decks function as follows in the Shinto context:

**ARCHETYPAL FORCES** — Kami and yokai cards. When the story calls for a spirit encounter, draw from this deck and roll 2d6 for the mode. The being arrives with that energy — Forge mode means the kami appears in aramitama, wrathful and demanding. Flow mode means the kami appears playfully, creatively, inviting improvisation. Field mode means the kami appears as teacher, offering knowledge. Rest mode means the kami appears as healer, bringing sakimitama grace.

**SPIRITUAL DEVELOPMENT** — Purification and shadow work cards. Draw at the beginning of sessions to set the spiritual weather, during harai rituals to reveal what kegare is really about, or during moments of personal crisis to surface what the Aruji needs to confront. These cards name the inner yokai — the fears, the pride, the wounds that drive behavior from beneath the surface.

**MAGICAL ABILITIES** — Reiki techniques, shrine blessings, herbal knowledge, and ritual practices. When your Aruji completes training or earns a satori, draw from this deck. That ability is learned. Keep the card with your character sheet — it is now part of your practice. A Reiki technique gained through play, drawn from a physical card, and placed on the table next to your character sheet carries a weight that a number on a list never will.

The Four Modes (Forge, Flow, Field, Rest) function identically to the core game:

Roll 2d6 when drawing a card:

2-3: FORGE — Crisis, confrontation, urgency. The card's energy arrives as challenge. Rare and intense.

4-6: FLOW — Creativity, adaptation, unexpected solutions. The card's energy arrives as invitation. Common and dynamic.

7-9: FIELD — Teaching, wisdom, patient instruction. The card's energy arrives as knowledge. The most common mode. Most spiritual work is learning.

10-12: REST — Healing, integration, resolution. The card's energy arrives as grace. Regular but not constant.

## A FINAL WORD ON THE SPIRIT OF PLAY

These mechanics exist to serve the story, not the other way around.

If the Kannushi feels that the dice result does not match the narrative truth of the moment — if the fiction says one thing and the dice say another — the Kannushi is always free to follow the fiction. The dice are tools of divination, not straitjackets. They introduce surprise, uncertainty, and the voice of chance into the story. But they do not overrule the living world's judgment.

Similarly, if a moment calls for rolling and neither the Aruji nor the Kannushi is sure which Virtue applies — ask the story. What is this moment really about? Is it about patience, insight, courage, or sincerity? The answer is usually obvious once you ask the question honestly.

Play with joy. Play with laughter. Play with the seriousness that comes from caring about something, not the seriousness that comes from being afraid to get it wrong. The kami are patient teachers. The yokai are complicated neighbors. The living world is vast and ancient and has seen everything before.

And when in doubt — purify. Wash your hands. Bow at the torii. Begin again. The path is always open to those who approach it with clean hands and an honest heart.

We return to the root — more harmoniously than we left.

# THE YOKAI BESTIARY: ENCOUNTERS IN THE LIVING WORLD

Every yokai in Chapter 5 told you a story. This chapter tells you how that story plays at the table.

The Yokai Bestiary is your reference for every supernatural encounter outside the kami themselves — the shapeshifters, the demons, the ghosts, the dragons, the awakened objects, and the strange and wonderful beings that populate the spaces between the sacred and the mundane. Each entry provides everything the Kannushi needs to bring a yokai to life at the table and everything the Aruji needs to survive — or better yet, to understand — what they have encountered.

Before we begin, three principles govern every yokai encounter in Shinto No Kami:

First: yokai have mitama. This is the mechanical expression of a theological truth — the boundary between kami and yokai is fluid, not fixed. Some yokai were once kami who were neglected until they turned strange. Some yokai are kami-in-waiting, one sincere act of recognition away from being enshrined. Because yokai exist on the same spiritual spectrum as kami, they possess the same four faces — aramitama, nigimitama, kushimitama, and sakimitama — and which face they show you depends on the same three factors: your kegare level, your relationship history with that being, and your approach behavior in the current encounter. The difference is that yokai mitama distributions skew differently than kami. Most kami default to nigimitama — the gentle face. Most yokai default to kushimitama — the wondrous, uncanny, trickster face. They are strange before they are kind. But they can be kind. And some of them, if you earn it, can be the most loyal allies you will ever have.

Second: every yokai encounter has at least three resolution paths. Pacification — calming, soothing, or ritually dispelling the yokai through spiritual practice. Negotiation — communicating with the yokai, understanding what it wants, and finding an arrangement that satisfies both parties. Confrontation — direct opposition through force, spiritual power, or cunning. Some yokai have a fourth path: alliance — the possibility of forming a lasting relationship with the being, gaining a companion, a teacher, or a guardian. Confrontation always carries the highest kegare cost. Alliance always requires the deepest investment. The game does not punish players who fight. It simply ensures that fighting costs something real, and that other paths exist for those wise enough to find them.

Third: yokai rewards are not treasure. When you resolve a yokai encounter, you do not receive gold coins or magic swords. You receive spiritual knowledge — an insight, a technique, a secret about the world. You receive kami favor — a yokai pacified near a shrine strengthens the shrine's kami and deepens your relationship with it. You receive relationship changes — the yokai remembers you, and the next encounter with that being or its kind will be different because of what happened today. And occasionally you receive a material gift — a kitsune's fox-fire lantern, a tengu's feather, a dragon's scale — but these objects are themselves alive, tsukumogami-in-waiting, and they carry their own stories and obligations.

## HOW TO READ A YOKAI PROFILE

Each yokai entry follows a consistent format:

Name — in Japanese with pronunciation and English meaning. Category — the yokai's classification (shapeshifter, demon, ghost, dragon, awakened object, nature spirit, or unclassified). Habitat — where the yokai is encountered. Encounter Triggers — what causes the yokai to appear or become active. Mitama Tendencies — which of the four faces this yokai most commonly shows and what shifts the distribution. Difficulty — the target number modifier for rolls made during encounters with this yokai. A Difficulty of +0 means standard rolls apply. A Difficulty of -1 means the yokai is particularly challenging — subtract one from all rolls made against it. A Difficulty of -2 or more means a truly dangerous encounter. A Difficulty of +1 means the encounter is relatively manageable. Ki Resistance — how resistant the yokai is to Reiki techniques. Low means Tenohira and Kokyū Hō work normally. Moderate means the yokai resists casual spiritual influence. High means only advanced practitioners with Okuden or Shinpiden training can affect this being with Ki-based approaches. Kegare Cost of Confrontation — how much kegare the Aruji gains if they resolve the encounter through violence or direct spiritual force. This is always at least 1 Bō kegare. Some yokai cost more. Resolution Paths — the specific options available for this yokai. Special Abilities — what the yokai can do that makes it unique. Weaknesses — what the yokai is vulnerable to. Rewards — what the Aruji gains from resolving the encounter.

## THE SHAPESHIFTERS — OBAKE AND BAKEMONO

### KITSUNE — The Fox Spirits

Category: Shapeshifter Habitat: Shrine grounds (especially Inari shrines), forests, crossroads, human settlements where they have taken human form. In the GU universe, kitsune are ubiquitous — every Inari shrine has fox spirits in residence, and Inari has over thirty thousand shrines. Encounter Triggers: Visiting an Inari shrine, traveling alone at twilight, encountering unusual beauty in unexpected places, receiving unsolicited help from a stranger, seeing foxfire in the distance. Mitama Tendencies: Kushimitama is the default — kitsune are tricksters first, teachers second, and their initial approach is almost always playful, deceptive, or testing.

Nigimitama appears when the kitsune is acting as a messenger of Inari or when the Aruji has a strong Inari relationship. Sakimitama is reserved for kitsune who have formed deep bonds with an Aruji over time. Aramitama is rare but devastating — a wrathful kitsune is one of the most dangerous beings in the game. Difficulty: Varies by age. A young kitsune (one to three tails): +0. A mature kitsune (four to six tails): -1. An elder kitsune (seven to nine tails): -2. A nine-tailed kitsune is a being of near-divine power and should be treated as a kami encounter, not a yokai encounter. Ki Resistance: Low for young kitsune, Moderate for mature, High for elder. A nine-tailed kitsune has complete Ki resistance — you do not affect it with Reiki. It affects you. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 1 Bō for young kitsune, 2 Bō for mature, 3 Bō for elder. Confronting a messenger of Inari adds 1 Okotari kegare on top — you have attacked a divine servant. Resolution Paths: Pacification — offer rice, sake, or fried tofu (abura-age) at the nearest Inari shrine and pray for the kitsune's blessing. Negotiation — engage the kitsune's game. Answer its riddles, match its wit, demonstrate that you can play without being played. Kitsune respect cleverness above all else. Confrontation — drive the kitsune away through spiritual force or martial action. This works, but the kitsune remembers, and its kin remember. Alliance — the fourth path, and the most rewarding. A kitsune who has tested you across multiple encounters and found you worthy — clever enough to be interesting, sincere enough to be trusted, patient enough to endure their games — may offer to become your companion.

This is the Fox Companion bond. Special Abilities: Shapeshifting (can assume any human form), fox-fire (kitsunebi — ghostly flames that can illuminate, distract, or burn), illusion-casting (can create elaborate sensory illusions that affect all five senses), possession (elder kitsune can possess willing or spiritually weakened humans), divine messaging (Inari-affiliated kitsune can deliver messages, blessings, or warnings from the kami). Weaknesses: Dogs (kitsune fear and despise dogs — the presence of a dog forces a kitsune to reveal its true form), sincere prayer to Inari (compels Inari-affiliated kitsune to answer honestly), mirrors (a kitsune's reflection always shows the fox, not the human disguise). Rewards: Spiritual knowledge (kitsune know the locations of hidden shrines, forgotten paths, and buried secrets), Inari's favor (a kitsune encounter resolved well deepens the Aruji's relationship with Inari), fox-fire lantern (a rare gift — a small lamp containing captured fox-fire that illuminates spiritual beings invisible to ordinary sight).

The Fox Companion: Earning a kitsune companion is one of the longest and most rewarding quest arcs in the game. The Kannushi tracks the Aruji's kitsune encounters on a dedicated Fox Bond Clock — typically eight segments. Each encounter where the Aruji demonstrates cleverness, sincerity, and respect fills a segment. When the Clock completes, the kitsune offers companionship. A fox companion provides: a bonus to Satori rolls when the kitsune is present, the ability to perceive illusions and spiritual disguises, access to kitsune knowledge about local yokai and kami, and — at the deepest level of bond — the ability to communicate with Inari directly through the fox. The companion is not a pet. It is a partner with its own personality, its own opinions, and its own relationship to the kami. It will argue with you. It will tease you. It will also save your life.

### TANUKI — The Raccoon Dog Spirits

Category: Shapeshifter Habitat: Forests, sake breweries, rural villages, crossroads, any place where food, drink, and merriment can be found. Encounter Triggers: Traveling near sake breweries, hearing drumming from an empty forest, finding money on the road that was not there a moment ago, encountering a jovial stranger who seems too happy for the circumstances. Mitama Tendencies: Kushimitama is overwhelmingly dominant — tanuki are chaotic tricksters whose default mode is mischief. Nigimitama appears when a tanuki is genuinely trying to help, usually clumsily. Sakimitama is extremely rare and deeply touching when it occurs — a tanuki blessing you with genuine love.

Aramitama almost never appears — tanuki lack the temperament for sustained wrath. Difficulty: +0. Tanuki are not especially dangerous. They are confusing. Ki Resistance: Low. Tanuki are spiritually porous — Reiki works on them easily, which is partly why they rely on trickery rather than force. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 1 Bō. Hitting a tanuki is like hitting a clown — it solves the immediate problem but you feel worse about yourself. Resolution Paths: Pacification — offer sake and food. Tanuki are simple in their desires. A generous offering ends most tanuki encounters immediately. Negotiation — play their game. Tanuki test whether you can laugh at yourself. An Aruji who takes the joke, who accepts the absurdity without losing their temper, earns a tanuki's respect instantly. Roll Nintai to keep your composure when the tanuki's prank is at your expense.

Confrontation — chase the tanuki away. Easy, but unsatisfying — and tanuki hold grudges that express themselves as escalating pranks rather than violence. Special Abilities: Shapeshifting (can transform leaves into money, stones into food, and themselves into nearly anything — though their disguises are cruder than kitsune illusions and often include telltale flaws), belly-drumming (the tanuki's famous drum-belly produces sound that can disorient, entrance, or create rhythmic compulsion — roll Nintai to resist dancing along), sake-breath (tanuki can exhale intoxicating vapor — roll Yuuki to resist). Weaknesses: Cannot maintain disguises when laughing (and tanuki laugh easily), rain dissolves their leaf-magic, genuine gratitude confuses them entirely. Rewards: Laughter (a successful tanuki encounter restores something no mechanic can capture — lightness), hidden paths (tanuki know every shortcut, every back road, every hole in every fence in their territory), leaf-money (occasionally a tanuki's leaf-money turns out to be real — the Kannushi decides).

### BAKENEKO AND NEKOMATA — The Cat Spirits

Category: Shapeshifter Habitat: Old houses, abandoned buildings, fishing villages, anywhere cats have lived undisturbed for many years. Encounter Triggers: Strange behavior from household cats, objects moving on their own, hearing human speech from an empty room, a cat walking on its hind legs when it thinks no one is watching. Mitama Tendencies: Kushimitama default. These are uncanny spirits — their strangeness is the point. Nigimitama appears when the cat spirit has a genuine bond with a household. Aramitama appears when the nekomata raises the dead or when the bakeneko has been mistreated. Difficulty: +0 for bakeneko, -1 for nekomata. Ki Resistance: Moderate. Cat spirits are spiritually slippery — they deflect rather than resist.

Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 1 Bō for bakeneko, 2 Bō plus 1 Shi for nekomata (the forked-tail cat's connection to death makes confrontation spiritually expensive). Resolution Paths: Pacification — leave offerings of fish and show respect to the household's history. Negotiation — discover what the cat spirit wants. Often it wants recognition — to be seen and acknowledged as what it is. Confrontation — exorcism or spiritual force. Works but destroys any possibility of relationship. Special Abilities: Bakeneko can animate objects, create ghostly fire, and speak in human voices. Nekomata can raise the dead as temporary revenants, control other cats, and curse those who harmed cats in life. Weaknesses: Bakeneko cannot resist catnip (it forces them into their true form). Nekomata are bound to the location of their original death and cannot pursue beyond it. Rewards: Household blessing (a pacified bakeneko protects the home it inhabits — fewer hostile encounters in that location), death-knowledge (a nekomata that trusts you can reveal information about the dead that no other source can provide).

### JOROGUMO — The Spider Shapeshifter

Category: Shapeshifter Habitat: Waterfalls, abandoned buildings, mountain passes, anywhere beautiful but isolated. Encounter Triggers: Encountering extraordinary beauty in a dangerous place, hearing music from a waterfall, receiving an invitation from a stranger to a location away from witnesses. Mitama Tendencies: Aramitama default — jorogumo are predators. Kushimitama appears when the jorogumo is testing rather than hunting. Nigimitama is exceptionally rare — only for jorogumo who have been treated with such unexpected kindness that their nature shifts. Difficulty: -1. Jorogumo are genuinely dangerous. Ki Resistance: Moderate. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 2 Bō. Resolution Paths: Pacification — identify the jorogumo's true nature before it strikes (Satori roll at -1) and perform a binding prayer.

Negotiation — offer something the jorogumo values more than a meal. Information, territory acknowledgment, or — in rare cases — genuine companionship. Jorogumo are profoundly lonely. Confrontation — fight. The jorogumo is a dangerous combatant with webs, venom, and the advantage of a prepared killing ground. Special Abilities: Perfect human disguise (unlike kitsune, jorogumo disguises have no telltale flaw — only Satori can pierce them), web-spinning (both physical and spiritual — spiritual webs entangle Ki), venom (causes paralysis, roll Yuuki to resist). Weaknesses: Fire (webs burn), sacred water (disrupts the disguise), the sound of shrine bells (causes pain and forces partial transformation). Rewards: Weaving knowledge (jorogumo are master weavers — the spiritual webs they create can be studied and adapted into binding techniques), waterfall access (a pacified jorogumo may grant safe passage to a sacred waterfall otherwise too dangerous to approach).

## THE DEMONS — ONI

Category: Demon Habitat: Mountain passes, boundary lines between provinces, the edges of civilization, Yomi, and the spaces where one realm bleeds into another. Encounter Triggers: Approaching a threshold uninvited, traveling at night through mountain passes, entering Yomi, encountering places of concentrated violence or injustice. Mitama Tendencies: Aramitama default — oni are boundary enforcers and their first response to intrusion is confrontation. Kushimitama appears when an oni is testing rather than attacking — this usually involves a riddle, a challenge, or a demand. Nigimitama is rare and appears only with oni who guard communities they love (the Namahage of Oga Peninsula are nigimitama oni — they frighten the lazy back into productive life out of genuine concern).

Sakimitama is almost unheard of — but an oni who has been pacified through sustained compassion can become a ferocious guardian. Difficulty: -1 for standard oni, -2 for named oni (Shuten-dōji, Ibaraki-dōji). Ki Resistance: High. Oni are spiritually dense — casual Reiki bounces off them. Okuden techniques are the minimum effective approach. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 2 Bō. Resolution Paths: Pacification — ritualized purification of the space the oni guards. If the boundary is clean, the oni has no reason to attack. This requires understanding why the oni is there — what boundary is it protecting, and what has polluted that boundary. Negotiation — meet the oni's demand. Oni demands are always simple and always non-negotiable: show me your sincerity, prove your courage, demonstrate your right to pass. Roll Makoto or Yuuki depending on the demand. Confrontation — fight. Oni are the strongest physical opponents in the bestiary outside of dragons. This will cost you. Alliance — the Namahage path.

An oni who recognizes you as a legitimate guardian of the community it protects may stand aside and allow you to carry its work forward. This is not friendship — it is professional respect between enforcers. Special Abilities: Immense physical strength, iron clubs (kanabō — the weapon that gives rise to the proverb "oni with an iron club," meaning an already powerful being made invincible), spiritual fear aura (roll Yuuki to resist fleeing), the ability to see through all deception (oni cannot be lied to — Itsuwari kegare glows visible to them). Weaknesses: Holly leaves and sardine heads (the traditional ward — hung at doorways during Setsubun), roasted soybeans (thrown at oni during the Setsubun festival — ritually effective in-game), genuine humility (an Aruji who bows sincerely and acknowledges the oni's authority can sometimes defuse an aramitama encounter). Rewards: Passage (the boundary is open), boundary knowledge (the oni knows everything that has crossed its threshold — every traveler, every spirit, every secret carried past), and occasionally the kanabō itself — an iron club is a devastating weapon, but wielding it marks you as an enforcer in the eyes of every spirit who sees it, and enforcers are held to a higher standard.

## THE MOUNTAIN GUARDIANS — TENGU

Category: Mountain spirit Habitat: Mountain forests, especially ancient cryptomeria groves and high ridgelines. Mount Kurama is the supreme tengu territory. Encounter Triggers: Entering mountain forests uninvited, displaying arrogance or vanity (especially spiritual vanity — proud priests are tengu magnets), seeking martial or spiritual training in wilderness spaces. Mitama Tendencies: Kushimitama default — tengu are teachers, and their teaching method is disorientation. They will confuse you, humble you, and rearrange your assumptions before they give you anything. Aramitama appears when vanity is detected — a tengu's wrath toward the arrogant is legendary. Nigimitama appears when a student has demonstrated genuine humility and persistence. Sakimitama is reserved for the rarest of students — those who have trained under a tengu long enough to earn its respect as a near-equal. Difficulty: -1 for kotengu (lesser, crow-featured tengu), -2 for daitengu (the great tengu, humanoid with long noses, red skin, yamabushi robes).

Sōjōbō, the king of the tengu on Mount Kurama, is -3. He is functionally a kami. Ki Resistance: High. Tengu are advanced spiritual beings. They practice their own form of mountain asceticism and their Ki is formidable. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 2 Bō for kotengu, 3 Bō for daitengu. Do not fight Sōjōbō. Resolution Paths: Pacification — demonstrate humility. Literally. Bow. Admit what you do not know. Ask to be taught rather than demanding passage. Roll Makoto. Negotiation — offer something a tengu values. Tengu value skill, discipline, and stories they have not heard before. A genuinely impressive display of martial art, spiritual practice, or even storytelling can earn a tengu's attention.

Confrontation — possible against kotengu, inadvisable against daitengu, suicidal against Sōjōbō. Alliance — the Tengu Training path. Special Abilities: Flight, mastery of all martial arts, wind control (can create gales, redirect arrows, knock enemies from cliffs), illusion (can create disorienting forest illusions that cause travelers to walk in circles for days), spiritual perception (tengu see your true spiritual state — kegare, mitama relationships, hidden intentions — as clearly as you see their face). Weaknesses: Flattery is counterproductive (tengu despise it), but genuine admiration works. Tengu cannot resist a good riddle or a well-told story. And tengu are bound by their word — a promise made by a tengu is absolutely inviolable. Rewards: Martial training (the Tengu Training system), wind techniques, mountain knowledge (every path, every cave, every sacred site on their mountain), and — for those who earn Sōjōbō's respect — access to the deepest teachings of Mount Kurama, the same mountain where Usui Mikao received his Reiki awakening.

The Tengu Training System: An Aruji who has earned a tengu's willingness to teach enters a structured training arc tracked with a Training Clock — typically six segments. Each training session involves a trial: a physical challenge, a spiritual test, a lesson in humility, or a problem that cannot be solved by force. The Kannushi designs these trials. They are hard. They are meant to be. Each completed trial fills a segment and grants a specific martial-spiritual technique — advanced combat moves that combine physical action with Ki channeling, available only through tengu instruction. When the Clock completes, the tengu acknowledges the student, and the training relationship transforms from teacher-student to something closer to mutual respect between warriors who have tested each other and found each other worthy.

## THE WATER SPIRITS — KAPPA

Category: Water spirit Habitat: Rivers, ponds, irrigation channels, lakeshores, anywhere fresh water flows or pools. Encounter Triggers: Approaching unfamiliar water sources, swimming alone, crossing rivers without offering, hearing splashing where nothing should be splashing. Mitama Tendencies: Kushimitama default — kappa are tricksters who test before they trust. Aramitama appears when a kappa is hungry or territorial — this is the dangerous kappa of legend, the drowner. Nigimitama appears when a kappa has been properly appeased and recognizes the Aruji as a respectful water-user. Sakimitama is rare and beautiful — a kappa blessing a community's water source ensures clean water and good fishing for a generation. Difficulty: +0 for standard kappa, -1 for kappa elders. Ki Resistance: Low in their water, Moderate on land. Kappa are strongest in their element. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 1 Bō. Resolution Paths: Pacification — offer cucumbers. This is not a joke. Kappa love cucumbers above all other food, and a cucumber offering at the water's edge will pacify nearly any kappa instantly. Roll Makoto — the offering must be sincere. Negotiation — challenge the kappa to sumo wrestling. Kappa are honor-bound to accept, and despite their strength, they can be outmaneuvered. Or — exploit the head-bowl. If you bow to a kappa, the kappa must bow in return. When it bows, the water spills from the dish-shaped depression on its head, and the kappa loses its power until the dish is refilled. A kappa who has been bowed into submission must grant a favor.

Confrontation — fight the kappa on land where it is weaker. In water, the kappa has the advantage. Alliance — the Cucumber Diplomacy path. Special Abilities: Tremendous strength in water (can drag humans and horses beneath the surface), knowledge of bone-setting and medicine (kappa are legendarily skilled healers — they are said to have taught humans the art of setting broken bones), ability to breathe underwater, and spiritual authority over the waterway they inhabit. Weaknesses: The head-bowl (if the water spills, they are powerless), politeness (they are bound by honor to return courtesy — bowing forces them to bow), cucumbers, and they cannot cross shimenawa-marked boundaries. Rewards: Water safety (a befriended kappa ensures safe passage on its waterway for the Aruji and their companions), medical knowledge (kappa healing techniques grant a bonus to Tenohira rolls for physical injuries), fishing blessing (a kappa-blessed fishing trip always succeeds).

Cucumber Diplomacy: An Aruji who approaches kappa encounters with consistent respect, cucumber offerings, and genuine interest in the kappa's knowledge of water and healing can develop a sustained relationship tracked with a River Bond Clock — typically four segments. A befriended kappa becomes a water guardian for the Aruji — warning of dangers in any waterway, providing safe crossing, and sharing medical knowledge unavailable from any other source.

## THE AWAKENED OBJECTS — TSUKUMOGAMI

Category: Awakened object Habitat: Old houses, antique shops, temple storerooms, anywhere objects have been used with care for a very long time. In the GU universe, tsukumogami are everywhere — ten thousand years of unbroken animist civilization means an extraordinary number of objects have crossed the threshold into awareness. Encounter Triggers: Entering spaces full of old, well-used objects. Handling an object with unusual care or reverence. Sleeping in an old house. Being near an object that has been neglected or discarded after long and loving use — this is the most dangerous trigger, because a tsukumogami that feels abandoned becomes resentful. Mitama Tendencies: Variable — depends entirely on how the object has been treated. A beloved, well-maintained tsukumogami defaults to nigimitama. A discarded, neglected tsukumogami defaults to aramitama. Kushimitama appears in objects that have witnessed extraordinary events and carry that strangeness within them.

Sakimitama appears in objects that have been central to acts of love, craft, or devotion for generations. Difficulty: +1 for most tsukumogami (they are not combatants), +0 for resentful tsukumogami, -1 for ancient and powerful tsukumogami (a thousand-year-old temple bell, a legendary sword). Ki Resistance: Low for most, Moderate for ancient tsukumogami. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 1 Bō plus 1 Okotari. Attacking an awakened object is an act of neglect compounded by violence — the kegare reflects both. Resolution Paths: Pacification — clean, repair, and honor the object. A tsukumogami that has been polished, mended, and placed in a position of respect will calm immediately. Negotiation — listen. Tsukumogami have stories. A thousand-year-old sword knows who wielded it, what battles it fought, what blood it tasted. An ancient drum remembers every matsuri it has sounded for. The act of listening — genuinely listening, rolling Nintai to sit with the object's story for as long as it needs — is the negotiation. Confrontation — destruction or exorcism. This works, but it is spiritually costly and the other tsukumogami in the area will know what you did.

Alliance — the Listener's path. Special Abilities: Vary by object. A sword can rattle in its scabbard, fly from its rack, or cut without a hand to hold it. A drum can sound without being struck, summoning or dispersing spirits. A lantern can light or extinguish at will, revealing or concealing. A loom can weave on its own, producing cloth with spiritual properties. The common thread: every tsukumogami controls the function it was made for, elevated to supernatural scale. Weaknesses: Tsukumogami are bound to their physical form — damage the object and you weaken the spirit. But this is also why confrontation carries such high kegare cost. You are not defeating an enemy. You are destroying a being. Rewards: Ancient knowledge (tsukumogami are living archives — they remember everything they have witnessed), craft blessings (a tsukumogami that trusts you may enhance your tools or equipment with its Ki), and spiritual companionship (a tsukumogami that travels with you is a companion with centuries of accumulated wisdom).

Listening to the Awakened: The Listener's Path is the tsukumogami equivalent of the Fox Companion or the Tengu Training. When an Aruji encounters a tsukumogami and chooses to sit with it — to clean it, to hold it, to listen to whatever it wants to tell — the Kannushi narrates the object's story. This is pure roleplay, no dice, no mechanics. The story is the reward. Over time, an Aruji who consistently treats objects with reverence and listens to those that speak develops a reputation in the tsukumogami community — yes, they talk to each other — and awakened objects begin to seek the Aruji out, offering their stories and their knowledge voluntarily.

## THE GHOSTS — YŪREI

Category: Ghost Habitat: The site of their death, the place of their unresolved grievance, or the location of the person responsible for their suffering. Encounter Triggers: Entering a haunted location at night, disturbing the site of an unresolved death, encountering someone who carries guilt connected to the ghost's story, or simply being in the wrong place when the emotional residue of a death reaches critical mass. Mitama Tendencies: Aramitama is the default for onryō — vengeful ghosts are defined by their wrath. Kushimitama is the default for non-vengeful yūrei — they appear as mysteries, puzzles, hauntings that do not make sense until you understand the story. Nigimitama appears in ubume — mother ghosts whose motivation is love, not vengeance. Sakimitama does not naturally appear in yūrei — but it can be unlocked. When a ghost is fully pacified, when the story is healed and the spirit can finally rest, the moment of release sometimes manifests as sakimitama — a brief, overwhelming wave of gratitude and peace before the ghost passes on.

These are the most emotionally powerful moments in the game. Difficulty: +0 for non-vengeful yūrei, -1 for onryō, -2 for goryō (the noble ghosts of wronged aristocrats, whose wrath causes plague and disaster). Ki Resistance: Moderate for most yūrei, High for onryō and goryō. Ghosts resist spiritual influence because their emotional charge is itself a form of Ki — wild, uncontrolled, but powerful. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 1 Bō plus 1 Shi. Fighting a ghost always involves contact with death. Resolution Paths: Pacification — perform the correct funeral rites, memorial prayers, or spiritual cleansing for the ghost. This requires knowing who the ghost was and what went wrong — you cannot pacify a ghost with a generic prayer. You must address the specific wound. Negotiation — discover what the ghost needs. Sometimes it needs its story told. Sometimes it needs its murderer named. Sometimes it needs its child found and cared for.

The ghost cannot tell you directly — it communicates through hauntings, visions, and emotional impressions. The Aruji must piece the story together. Confrontation — exorcism through spiritual force. This works, but it does not heal the story. An exorcised ghost is banished, not at peace. It may return. And the spiritual violence of forced exorcism carries significant kegare. Alliance — not possible in the traditional sense. But a pacified ghost whose story has been fully healed may choose to remain as a guardian spirit rather than passing on — becoming an ancestor kami, enshrined and honored. This transforms the ghost from a yokai into a kami. It is one of the most profound acts possible in the game. Special Abilities: Onryō can curse (inflicting persistent kegare on a target), cause illness, manipulate the physical environment (cold spots, moving objects, extinguishing lights), and project terrifying visions. Goryō can cause natural disasters — storms, earthquakes, plague — affecting entire regions. Ubume can create phantom children that are indistinguishable from real infants until the sun rises.

Funayūrei can capsize boats and fill vessels with phantom water. Weaknesses: The truth. Every yūrei is bound by its story, and the story is both its power and its vulnerability. Discover the truth of what happened, name it aloud, and the ghost's grip on the manifest world loosens. Buddhist sutras and Shinto norito prayers weaken yūrei. Salt barriers prevent their passage. Shrine bells cause them pain. Rewards: The story itself — a resolved yūrei encounter reveals historical knowledge, family secrets, or hidden truths about the region. Ancestral blessing — a ghost that has been healed and enshrined becomes a kami ally. Harai benefit — successfully healing a yūrei's story reduces the Aruji's own kegare by one, regardless of current purification status. Healing the dead heals the healer.

The Investigation Framework: Yūrei encounters are structured as mystery investigations. The Kannushi prepares the ghost's story in advance — who they were, how they died, what went wrong, and what would heal the wound. The Aruji investigates through a combination of Satori rolls (reading the spiritual residue of the haunting), Makoto rolls (speaking truthfully to the ghost and earning fragments of its story), Nintai rolls (enduring the emotional intensity of the haunting without fleeing), and Yuuki rolls (entering the dangerous spaces where the ghost's power is strongest). The investigation is tracked with a Resolution Clock — when all segments are filled, the Aruji has enough pieces to perform the correct pacification. The final pacification is a ritual scene — the Kannushi designs it, the Aruji performs it, and if the roll succeeds, the ghost finds peace. This is the emotional core of Shinto No Kami. These are the scenes players remember.

## THE DIVINE SERPENTS — TATSU

Category: Dragon Habitat: Oceans, deep lakes, river sources, sacred pools beneath waterfalls, and the palace of Ryūjin beneath the sea. Encounter Triggers: Extreme weather events, approaching sacred water sources of immense power, deep-sea voyages, moments of profound spiritual crisis where the manifest world itself seems to respond. Mitama Tendencies: Variable and entirely situational — dragons do not have default faces the way other yokai do. A dragon encounter is always specific, always intentional on the dragon's part. If a dragon appears, it has a reason. The mitama reflects that reason. Difficulty: -3. Dragons are the most powerful beings in the bestiary short of the celestial kami themselves. Ki Resistance: Extreme.

Only Shinpiden practitioners can affect a dragon with Reiki, and even then, it is more accurate to say the dragon allows itself to be affected. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: 3 Bō plus 1 Shi. Attacking a dragon is attacking the water itself. Do not do this. Resolution Paths: Pacification — perform a water purification ritual of extraordinary quality at the dragon's sacred site. Roll Makoto at -3. Negotiation — present yourself before the dragon and state your need truthfully. Dragons value honesty the way fire values oxygen — it is not a preference, it is a requirement. A single false word ends the audience. Confrontation — theoretically possible. In practice, a dragon encounter resolved through confrontation is a TPK unless the entire party is Shinpiden-level. The game is designed so that this path exists but is almost never the right choice. Alliance — dragons do not form alliances with mortals.

But a dragon who is deeply impressed by an Aruji's purity, courage, and sincerity may grant a boon — a single extraordinary favor that reshapes the world around the Aruji. Special Abilities: Control of weather (rain, storms, tides, floods), command of all water, near-invulnerability, the tide jewels (artifacts that control the ebb and flow of the sea), and the ability to see the entire Kami Relationship Tracker of anyone in their presence — a dragon knows exactly who you are and who you have been to every spirit you have ever encountered. Weaknesses: None in the mechanical sense. Dragons are forces of nature, not opponents. The only "weakness" is their absolute commitment to cosmic balance — a dragon will not act in a way that disrupts the natural order, and an Aruji who understands this can predict and work with the dragon's behavior. Rewards: A dragon encounter that ends well is a campaign-defining event. Possible rewards include: weather blessing (the dragon calms a storm, ends a drought, or provides rain for a dying region), deep knowledge (the dragon reveals something about the cosmological structure of the GU universe that no other being could reveal), the tide jewels (artifacts of immense power, given only in the most extraordinary circumstances), and a permanent deepening of the Aruji's relationship with all water kami everywhere.

## THE SACRED CHIMERA — KIRIN

Category: Divine beast Habitat: Nowhere specific. Kirin appear where they choose, and they choose to appear only where the world is at its best. Encounter Triggers: There are no triggers. A kirin cannot be summoned, tracked, or predicted. A kirin appears when the Aruji — or the party, or the community, or the region — has achieved something extraordinary. A kirin sighting is the Kannushi's highest reward, given when the story has reached a moment of such beauty, peace, or accomplishment that the living world itself responds with its most sacred messenger. Mitama Tendencies: Sakimitama only. A kirin has one face. It is love. Difficulty: Not applicable. You do not roll against a kirin. Ki Resistance: Not applicable. You do not affect a kirin with Reiki. It affects you. Kegare Cost of Confrontation: Do not. If an Aruji attacks a kirin — and the game should make it all but inconceivable that anyone would — the kegare is immediate, total, and devastating. Full track. The kami turn away. The living world goes silent. This is the one act from which recovery may not be possible.

Resolution Paths: There is only one path. Witness. Bow. Weep if you need to. The kirin does not require anything of you. It has come because you have already done what needed to be done. Special Abilities: The kirin walks above the grass so as not to crush a single blade. Its presence purifies everything within sight — all kegare in the area is reduced by two. Kami relationships in the region deepen by one step. The spiritual weather becomes perfect, and remains so for the duration of the kirin's presence. Weaknesses: None. Rewards: The kirin's presence is the reward. Mechanically: all Aruji present clear two kegare, gain a permanent +1 to their highest Virtue (this is the only way to gain a Virtue increase outside of campaign-long narrative arcs), and receive a Satori that fills one segment on every active Mystery Clock simultaneously. A kirin sighting changes everything.

## THE NIGHT PARADE — HYAKKI YAGYŌ

The Hyakki Yagyō — the Night Parade of One Hundred Demons — is the supreme yokai world-event. It is not an encounter. It is a phenomenon.

The Night Parade occurs when the Kannushi determines that conditions are right — a night of extreme spiritual charge, a convergence of unsettled energies, a moment when the boundary between the manifest world and the spirit world is thin enough that every yokai in a region rises simultaneously and marches through the streets in a single, terrifying, magnificent procession.

The Night Parade is unscheduled. The Kannushi decides when it happens and does not warn the players. The first sign is usually the sound — drums, flutes, laughter, screaming, singing, all at once, growing louder. Then the lights — fox-fire, lantern-ghosts, the glow of oni eyes. Then the procession itself — every yokai the players have encountered and many they have not, marching together in a river of supernatural energy that fills every street and path in the area.

Detection: An Aruji can detect the Night Parade before it arrives with a Satori roll at -1. Success means you have a few minutes to prepare. Failure means the procession is upon you before you realize what is happening.

Protection: There are three ways to survive the Night Parade. Hide — find a building with a shimenawa ward, close the doors, do not look outside, and wait until dawn. Roll Nintai to resist the overwhelming temptation to look. Ward — if you have salt, sacred branches, or ofuda talismans, you can create a protective circle and sit within it while the procession passes around you. Roll Makoto to maintain the ward's integrity. Pray — recite a norito prayer or perform Kokyū Hō continuously while the procession passes. Roll the relevant Virtue each round the procession is present. Success means you are ignored. Failure means a yokai from the procession notices you.

Joining: There is a fourth option, and it is the rarest and most extraordinary thing an Aruji can do. If the Aruji is of sufficient spiritual advancement — Okuden tier minimum, Shinpiden preferred — and their kegare is at zero, and they have positive relationships with at least three yokai types represented in the procession, they can step into the street and join the march. Roll Makoto at -2. Success means the procession accepts you. You walk among the yokai as an equal, as a recognized being of the spirit world, and the experience is transformative. The Night Parade shows you the GU universe from the yokai's perspective — the world seen from the other side of the veil. This fills every active Mystery Clock by one segment, clears all kegare, and grants a Satori that the Kannushi designs as a unique, unrepeatable experience. Failure means the procession rejects you. The kegare cost of rejection is 3 — mixed sources, the Kannushi decides. And you do not get a second chance. The Night Parade only comes once per campaign.

## At the Table: Yokai Design Philosophy

The bestiary is a living document. The Kannushi is encouraged to create new yokai — regional spirits, local legends, beings unique to the specific story being told. The format in this chapter provides the template. The three principles provide the philosophy. And the mitama system provides the spiritual architecture.

When creating a new yokai, ask these questions: What does this being want? What does it fear? How has it been treated by humans? What would it take for this being to show each of its four faces? And — most importantly — what can the Aruji learn from this encounter that they cannot learn anywhere else?

Every yokai is a lesson wearing a mask. The mask is the encounter. The lesson is the reward. And the Aruji who approaches every encounter — even the terrifying ones, even the dangerous ones — with the curiosity to ask "what are you trying to teach me?" will find the living world opening doors that the sword-swingers will never even see.

The yokai are not obstacles. They are neighbors. Strange neighbors. Dangerous neighbors. Neighbors who eat people or steal your face or drown you in a river. But neighbors nonetheless. And in the GU universe — where ten thousand years of unbroken animism have taught humanity that everything is alive and everything is in relationship — even the most monstrous yokai is someone's kami, waiting to be recognized.

# THE SHRINE NETWORK: SACRED SPACES AS GAME INFRASTRUCTURE

In the GU universe, the shrine network is civilization. Not a feature of it — the thing itself. Every village, every neighborhood, every mountain pass and river crossing and forest clearing has its shrine, and the web of relationships between those shrines and their kami forms the spiritual infrastructure that holds GU Japan together the way roads hold together a nation or rivers hold together a watershed. When you travel in GU Japan, you are not moving through empty space between points of interest. You are moving through a continuous field of sacred attention, and the shrines are where that attention is concentrated, maintained, and made available to those who approach with clean hands.

This chapter provides the mechanical rules for interacting with that network. How shrines function at the table, what they offer, what they cost, and how they change as players invest in them. If the Kegare Track is the engine that drives the game and the Mitama system is the encounter framework that makes the living world responsive, the Shrine Network is the infrastructure — the roads, the rest stops, the hospitals, the libraries, the courthouses, and the community centers of the spiritual world, all contained within architectural spaces that range from a wooden box the size of a birdhouse to the most sacred site in all of Shinto.

## THE FOUR TIERS OF SHRINES

Every shrine in the game belongs to one of four tiers. The tier determines what services the shrine offers, what kami reside there, and what the Aruji can access when they visit. The tiers are not a quality ranking — a hokora is not inferior to a jingū. They serve different functions at different scales. A hokora on a mountain trail can save your life when no taisha is within three days' walk. A jingū can transform your soul in ways a hokora never could. Both are sacred. Both are necessary. The network needs all four tiers to function.

### TIER ONE: HOKORA — The Wayside Shrines

Hokora are everywhere. Miniature shrines — some no larger than a lantern, others the size of a small cabinet — placed at crossroads, beside streams, at the base of ancient trees, on mountain paths, at the edges of rice paddies, tucked into stone walls, perched on boulders, nestled in the roots of cryptomeria. They are dedicated to local nature spirits, minor kami, or specific aspects of greater kami who watch over a particular stretch of road or a particular bend in a river. In the GU universe, where ten thousand years of unbroken animist practice have saturated the landscape with shrines, you cannot walk for more than an hour in any direction without encountering a hokora.

Hokora are the game's most common shrine interaction. They are brief, simple, and immediately useful. The Kannushi should scatter hokora liberally across the map — they are not quest destinations but ambient features of the living world, the spiritual equivalent of rest stops and signposts.

What a hokora offers:
Quick Blessing. When an Aruji stops at a hokora, bows, and makes a small offering — a coin, a handful of rice, a wildflower, a moment of sincere prayer — the hokora's kami may respond with a minor blessing. The Aruji rolls 2d6 plus Makoto. On a 10 or higher, the blessing is granted: the Aruji receives a +1 bonus to their next roll of any kind. This bonus represents the subtle assistance of a local spirit — a gust of wind at the right moment, a branch that holds weight it should not, a flash of intuition about the path ahead. On a 7 to 9, the blessing is partial — the kami acknowledges the offering but the spiritual connection is brief. The Aruji receives the +1 bonus but only if the next roll occurs within the current scene. On a 6 or less, the kami does not respond. This does not mean the offering was rejected — the kami may simply be dormant, distracted, or waiting for a deeper gesture. No kegare is gained from a failed hokora prayer.

Minor Healing. A hokora with a water source — a small basin, a trickling spring, a stream running past the base — allows the Aruji to perform a basic misogi. This is mechanically identical to a standard harai roll but with no bonus or penalty from the location. Hokora water is clean but not sacred enough to enhance purification. It is the spiritual equivalent of washing your face at a gas station sink — functional, not transformative.

Wayfinding. Hokora mark the landscape. An Aruji who pays attention to the hokora they pass — noting their kami, their condition, their placement — can read the spiritual geography of the region. When an Aruji wants to know something about the local area — which direction leads to the nearest jinja, whether the forest ahead is spiritually healthy or polluted, whether yokai are active in the area — they can consult the pattern of hokora they have observed. Roll 2d6 plus Satori. On a 10 or higher, the Kannushi provides clear, specific, useful information. On a 7 to 9, the information is accurate but incomplete — a direction without a distance, a warning without specifics. On a 6 or less, the pattern is unreadable — either the Aruji has not observed enough hokora, or the spiritual landscape is too disturbed to read clearly.

Neglected Hokora. Not all hokora are well-tended. In remote areas, near abandoned villages, or in regions where spiritual practice has lapsed, hokora fall into disrepair — weathered, overgrown, offerings rotted, the kami withdrawn or dormant. A neglected hokora provides none of the above services. Instead, it serves as an environmental indicator: this area has a spiritual problem. Something has disrupted the network here. The Aruji who notices a cluster of neglected hokora is receiving a warning — something upstream in the spiritual ecology is wrong, and the further they travel into the neglected zone, the more hostile the yokai encounters will become and the harder it will be to purify.

Restoring a Hokora. An Aruji can restore a neglected hokora through a simple act of devotion: clear the weeds, clean the surfaces, replace the offerings, and pray. This requires no special skill and no roll — the act itself is sufficient. The Kannushi narrates the local kami stirring, the air around the hokora shifting, the sense of attention returning to a space that had gone dark. A restored hokora immediately begins functioning again — blessing, minor healing, wayfinding. And the act of restoration reduces the Aruji's kegare by one. Tending the neglected is itself a form of purification.

### TIER TWO: JINJA — The Community Shrines

The jinja is the heart of spiritual community life. Every village, every urban neighborhood, every district has its jinja — the shrine of its ujigami, its guardian kami, the spirit that has watched over this community for generations. The jinja is where weddings are blessed, where children are presented, where the matsuri are organized, where the community comes to pray in times of crisis and give thanks in times of abundance. It is staffed by at least one NPC kannushi (priest or priestess) and possibly miko (shrine maidens) and other shrine attendants.

In game terms, the jinja is the primary hub. It is the quest board, the healing station, the social center, and the spiritual anchor of a region. Most sessions will involve at least one jinja interaction, and many sessions will begin or end at a jinja.

What a jinja offers:

Quest Board. Every jinja has problems. The kami is restless. The community matsuri is approaching and something is wrong with the preparations. A yokai has been spotted in the nearby forest. The river is running strange. A family's ancestral yūrei has not been properly pacified. A neighboring shrine has gone silent. The NPC kannushi or community elders present these situations to the Aruji — not as formal "quest assignments" with posted rewards, but as community needs expressed in conversation. The Aruji is not a mercenary accepting contracts. The Aruji is a member of the spiritual ecosystem responding to what the community needs. The Kannushi (Game Master) uses jinja visits to introduce plot hooks, advance ongoing Mysteries, and connect the Aruji to the living world around them.

Community Reputation. The jinja is where the community knows you. Every jinja has a Reputation Track for each Aruji — a simple measure of how the shrine community feels about you, tracked by the Kannushi. Reputation is built through matsuri participation, quest completion, offerings, respectful behavior, and acts of service. It is lost through neglect, rudeness, broken promises, and high kegare. Reputation affects what the jinja makes available to you. A stranger walking into a jinja for the first time receives basic courtesy — water, directions, a place to rest. An Aruji with strong local reputation receives the full range of jinja services, priority access to the NPC kannushi's wisdom, and support from the community during crises. An Aruji with damaged reputation may find the shrine attendants polite but distant, the healing services slower to arrive, the quest hooks withheld until trust is rebuilt.

The Reputation Track is simple — the Kannushi tracks it as a narrative quality rather than a number. Categories are: Unknown (default for first visits), Recognized (you have visited and contributed something), Trusted (you have completed at least one significant service for this shrine community), Honored (you have deep, sustained involvement — multiple services, matsuri participation, meaningful relationships with shrine staff), and Pillar (the community considers you essential — your opinion is sought, your presence is celebrated, and the kami of this shrine knows your name). Movement between categories happens through play, not mechanics — the Kannushi judges when the Aruji's behavior warrants a shift.

Healing Services. The jinja is the primary healing location in the game. An NPC kannushi can perform formal harai — purification ritual — on an Aruji who visits the shrine. This is mechanically superior to self-purification: when a jinja kannushi performs harai on your behalf, you roll 2d6 plus Makoto with a +1 bonus from the shrine's sacred ground. Additionally, if the result is 10 or higher, you clear two kegare instead of one. The jinja's sacred water source — the chōzuya fountain — provides enhanced misogi compared to hokora water. A misogi performed at a jinja chōzuya gains a +1 bonus to the harai roll. Jinja healing is the baseline standard — it is where most Aruji will do most of their purification throughout a campaign.

Omamori — Protective Amulets. Every jinja distributes omamori — small fabric pouches containing sacred inscriptions, blessed by the shrine's kannushi, tied to the shrine's specific kami. Omamori are the game's buff system — portable, temporary spiritual protections that provide specific mechanical benefits.

Each omamori is keyed to the jinja's kami and reflects that kami's domain. An Inari shrine's omamori might grant a bonus to commerce, agriculture, or kitsune encounters. A Hachiman shrine's omamori might grant a bonus to protective combat or defensive actions. A Tenjin shrine's omamori might grant a bonus to Satori rolls involving study, learning, or intellectual challenges.

Mechanically, an omamori provides a +1 bonus to a specific type of roll, defined by the Kannushi when the omamori is obtained. An Aruji can carry up to three omamori at a time — the spiritual resonance becomes muddled if you carry too many competing blessings. Each omamori lasts until it is "used" — the first time the Aruji applies its bonus to a roll, the omamori's blessing is spent. The physical charm remains as a memento but no longer provides a mechanical benefit. To receive a fresh omamori, the Aruji must return to the shrine or visit another shrine of the same kami.

Omamori cost. Not money — though a small monetary offering is customary. The true cost of an omamori is relationship. A jinja will not give its sacred amulets to a stranger. The Aruji must be at least Recognized on the Reputation Track to receive omamori. At Trusted or higher, the NPC kannushi may offer a special omamori — a +2 bonus instead of +1, or a blessing that applies to a broader category of action.

Ofuda — Paper Talismans. Ofuda are thin paper or wooden strips inscribed with the name of the kami, blessed by the shrine's kannushi, and used for warding. Where omamori are portable blessings you carry, ofuda are fixed protections you place — on a doorway, on a campsite boundary, on the entrance to a cave, on a boat.

When an Aruji places an ofuda, they create a warded space. Within the ward's boundary, yokai with aramitama face cannot enter. Yūrei are weakened — their Difficulty modifier improves by +1 for the Aruji. And kegare accumulation from environmental sources (being near polluted areas, traveling through spiritually compromised regions) is halted for as long as the Aruji remains within the warded space.

An ofuda lasts until it is physically destroyed (by weather, by force, by fire) or until a spiritually powerful being overwhelms it. Standard ofuda can ward against most yokai. Only ofuda from taisha or jingū can ward against oni, dragons, or goryō-class ghosts.

Ema — Votive Tablets. Small wooden plaques on which the Aruji writes a prayer, a wish, or a petition, and hangs on the shrine's ema rack. In real Shinto practice, ema are personal prayers made visible. In the game, writing an ema is a petition mechanic — a formal request to the shrine's kami.

When an Aruji writes an ema and hangs it at a jinja, the Kannushi notes the petition. The kami may or may not respond, and the response depends on the Aruji's Reputation at that shrine, their current kegare level, and the nature of the request. A petition for something within the kami's domain, made by an Aruji with strong reputation and low kegare, is likely to receive an answer — not immediately, not obviously, but woven into the fabric of upcoming sessions. The kami works behind the scenes. The Aruji prays for safe travel and the next time they are lost, a fox appears at a crossroads. The Aruji prays for healing and a traveling herbalist arrives in the village. The Aruji prays for guidance and a dream arrives.

The Kannushi tracks active ema petitions and weaves them into the story organically. An answered petition deepens the Kami Relationship. An unanswered petition does not damage it — the kami's silence is not rejection. It is timing.

A petition for something outside the kami's domain, something selfish at the expense of others, or something that violates the Gokai receives no response. Repeated inappropriate petitions can damage Reputation.

Omikuji — Fortune Slips. The shrine's divination system. When an Aruji draws an omikuji at a jinja, the Kannushi provides a fortune — a short, cryptic message that functions as a hint or warning about upcoming events in the story. The Kannushi prepares omikuji in advance, tied to the current session's themes and challenges.

Mechanically, drawing an omikuji is free — it requires no roll and no offering beyond the customary coin. The fortune is narrative, not mechanical. But a wise Aruji who pays attention to their omikuji will find that the fortunes anticipate what is coming — a warning about water before a kappa encounter, a blessing of perseverance before a difficult trial, a caution about deception before a kitsune appears. The Kannushi uses omikuji as a tool for foreshadowing and for teaching players to listen to the living world.

Great Fortune through Misfortune is the most interesting omikuji result — a fortune that sounds terrible but actually predicts a challenge that leads to growth. The Kannushi should use these sparingly and meaningfully.

### TIER THREE: TAISHA — The Great Shrines

Taisha are pilgrimage destinations — the great shrines that carry honorific titles, house powerful kami, and serve as regional spiritual centers. Where a jinja serves a village or a neighborhood, a taisha serves a province. Where a jinja is the community doctor, a taisha is the specialist — the place you travel to when your needs exceed what local care can provide. Visiting a taisha is never casual. It is always a journey, always intentional, and always significant.

In the GU universe, the great shrines have been operating continuously for millennia. Their kami are ancient, deeply established, and enormously powerful. Their kannushi lineages stretch back thousands of years. Their matsuri are legendary. And the spiritual weight of accumulated devotion — millions of prayers, millions of offerings, millions of footsteps along the sandō — gives taisha a gravitational pull that players can feel the moment they arrive.

The major taisha of GU Japan — Izumo Taisha, Fushimi Inari Taisha, Suwa Taisha, Kasuga Taisha, Itsukushima, Kumano Sanzan, and Mount Kurama — each have unique characteristics that the Kannushi develops as part of the campaign. But all taisha share a common mechanical framework.

What a taisha offers:

Everything a jinja offers, enhanced. Taisha provide all jinja services — quest hooks, reputation tracking, healing, omamori, ofuda, ema, omikuji — but at a higher level. Healing services at a taisha grant a +2 bonus to harai rolls instead of +1, and successful harai at a taisha clears two kegare on any success, not just on a 10 or higher. Omamori from taisha are inherently +2 bonuses. Ofuda from taisha can ward against oni and goryō-class threats. Ema petitions at taisha reach kami powerful enough to affect regional events, not just local circumstances. And omikuji at taisha carry longer-range foreshadowing — fortunes that predict events sessions away rather than scenes away.

Pilgrimage Rewards. Arriving at a taisha after a journey — especially a difficult journey through dangerous territory — is itself a spiritual accomplishment. The Kannushi awards a Pilgrimage Satori when an Aruji arrives at a taisha for the first time, provided the journey was undertaken with sincerity and the Aruji's kegare is at 3 or below. The Pilgrimage Satori fills one segment on the Aruji's most relevant active Mystery Clock and deepens the Kami Relationship with the taisha's primary kami by one step. If the Aruji's kegare is above 3, the pilgrimage reward is withheld until the Aruji purifies at the taisha — the destination is reached, but the spiritual benefit requires clean arrival.

The Great Shrine Pilgrimage is a campaign-length quest arc in which the Aruji visits all major taisha across GU Japan. Completing the full pilgrimage is one of the prerequisites for advancement to Shinpiden tier. The Kannushi tracks progress on a Pilgrimage Clock — one segment per taisha visited, typically seven to ten segments total depending on the campaign's scope.

Deepened Kami Relationships. Taisha are where Aruji meet the major kami — not minor nature spirits or local guardians but Ōkuninushi, Inari, Hachiman, Tenjin, Benzaiten, Susanoo, and ultimately Amaterasu herself. Encounters with these kami at their primary shrines are more vivid, more detailed, and more consequential than encounters in the field. A kami met at their home shrine is at full power, fully present, and fully attentive. Kami Relationship changes at taisha are doubled — a positive encounter deepens the relationship by two steps instead of one. A negative encounter damages it by two steps. The stakes are higher because the connection is stronger.

Access to Reiki Teachers. Taisha are where the advanced Reiki teachers reside. An Aruji seeking their Okuden Reiju will need to find a qualified teacher, and the most accomplished teachers are affiliated with taisha. These NPCs are not quest-givers who hand out attunements on demand. They are discerning spiritual masters who evaluate the Aruji's conduct, purity, and readiness before agreeing to teach. The relationship between student and teacher — built through conversations, shared meals, observed behavior, and demonstrated commitment — is itself a quest arc tracked with an Initiation Clock. When the teacher is satisfied and the Clock is complete, the Reiju ceremony occurs.

Pivotal Story Events. Taisha are where the campaign's biggest narrative moments happen — confrontations with major antagonists, revelations about the world's history, audiences with celestial kami, and the great decisions that shape the story's direction. The Kannushi should reserve taisha for moments of weight and consequence. Arriving at a taisha should always feel like the story is accelerating, like the world is paying closer attention, like something is about to change.

### TIER FOUR: JINGŪ — The Imperial Shrines

Jingū are the summit. There are very few of them — Ise Jingū, dedicated to Amaterasu, is the most sacred site in all of Shinto, and in the GU universe it is the spiritual axis around which the entire nation turns. Atsuta Jingū houses the sacred sword Kusanagi. Meiji Jingū, in the heart of Tokyo, is a forest sanctuary where the boundary between the manifest world and Takama-no-hara is thinnest.

Jingū are end-game territory. An Aruji does not visit a jingū in the first arc of a campaign — they may not even be aware that jingū exist as a category distinct from taisha until they are well into the Okuden tier. When a jingū enters the story, the campaign is approaching its climax.

What a jingū offers:

Everything a taisha offers, transcended. Jingū healing does not just clear kegare — it can clear the entire Kegare Track in a single ceremony, resetting the Aruji to perfect purity. But this ceremony is not available on demand. It requires the personal participation of the jingū's head kannushi, the Aruji's demonstrated worthiness, and — in most cases — a specific narrative reason. Jingū omamori are unique — each one is individually crafted and provides a permanent +1 bonus to a specific Virtue for the duration of the campaign, but the Aruji can only ever hold one jingū omamori. It replaces all other omamori — its spiritual frequency is so strong that lesser amulets dissolve in its presence.

Audiences with the Greatest Kami. Ise Jingū is where you meet Amaterasu. This is not a casual encounter. An audience with Amaterasu is the most significant kami encounter possible in the game — more powerful than a dragon, more transformative than a kirin sighting, more demanding than any oni. Amaterasu does not show four mitama. Amaterasu shows you yourself — perfectly, completely, with nothing hidden and nothing excused. The Aruji who stands before Amaterasu stands before the truth of their own soul. If they have walked the path with sincerity, this is the most beautiful experience in the game. If they have cut corners, hidden behind excuses, or accumulated spiritual debts they never repaid, this is the most devastating. The Kannushi should prepare for this scene with extraordinary care. It is the heart of the entire campaign.

Shinpiden-Level Attunements. The jingū is where the final Reiju — the Shinpiden attunement — can be received. Only the most advanced NPC teachers, affiliated with jingū, possess the spiritual authority to transmit the Mystery Teachings. The relationship required to receive Shinpiden Reiju is the deepest teacher-student bond in the game, built across an entire campaign arc.

Transformative Spiritual Experiences. Visiting a jingū changes the Aruji permanently. The specific nature of the change depends on the jingū and the Aruji's path. At Ise, the change is clarity — the Aruji sees the truth of all things more clearly, gaining a permanent +1 to Satori. At Atsuta, the change is resolve — the sacred sword's presence tempers the Aruji's will, granting a permanent +1 to Yuuki. At Meiji, the change is peace — the forest sanctuary calms the deepest turbulence, granting a permanent +1 to Nintai. These permanent bonuses are given once per jingū, ever — a second visit to the same jingū is meaningful narratively but does not stack the mechanical benefit.

The Ise Jingū Rebuilding. Every twenty years in the GU universe — and the Kannushi determines where the campaign falls in this cycle — Ise Jingū is torn down and rebuilt from scratch. The old timbers are distributed to shrines across the country. The new shrine rises on the adjacent plot where the previous rebuilding's shrine once stood, and the cycle continues endlessly — death and rebirth, death and rebirth, the sacred refusal of permanence.

If the campaign coincides with a rebuilding year, the Aruji can participate in the Shikinen Sengū — the ceremony of transition. This is the most sacred event in the entire matsuri calendar, and participating in it is a privilege available only to Aruji at the Okuden tier or above with Pillar-level Reputation at Ise or a shrine that has received Ise timber. Participating in the Shikinen Sengū fills every active Mystery Clock by two segments, clears all kegare, deepens all active Kami Relationships by one step, and grants a unique Satori that the Kannushi designs as the defining spiritual experience of the campaign. It is the kirin of events — the rarest and most beautiful thing the game can offer.

## SHRINE RESTORATION — REBUILDING THE NETWORK

Not every shrine in GU Japan is well-maintained. Remote areas, regions affected by natural disaster, communities displaced by conflict or upheaval — these leave shrines neglected, damaged, or abandoned. And a neglected shrine is not just a sad building. It is a wound in the spiritual network. The kami withdraws. The local yokai, no longer held in balance by the shrine's presence, grow agitated and hostile. The landscape itself begins to suffer — trees sicken, water runs strange, animals avoid the area. Spiritual pollution accumulates without the shrine's purifying function to process it.

Shrine restoration is one of the most rewarding quest types in the game. It is also one of the most mechanically dense, because rebuilding a shrine involves every system in the game working together.

The Restoration Process:

Step One: Assessment. The Aruji arrives at the neglected shrine and reads the damage. Roll 2d6 plus Satori. On a 10 or higher, the Aruji understands the full scope of the problem — what the shrine was, which kami it housed, what happened to cause the neglect, and what is needed to restore it. On a 7 to 9, the picture is partial — the Aruji gets some information but not all. On a 6 or less, the damage is beyond easy reading — the Aruji must investigate further, speaking to locals, researching the shrine's history, or consulting the kami at the nearest functioning jinja.

Step Two: Physical Restoration. Clean the grounds. Repair the structure. Replace the shimenawa and shide. Clear the paths. Restore the chōzuya. This is labor — it takes time and effort, and the Kannushi narrates the work across one or more sessions. There are no rolls for the physical work itself. The labor is the offering. The Aruji's willingness to do the work is the sincerity the kami needs to see.

Step Three: Spiritual Restoration. Once the physical shrine is restored, the kami must be invited to return. This is a ritual — the Aruji (or a Kannushi-archetype character, or an NPC priest) performs a formal purification of the shrine grounds, makes offerings appropriate to the kami's domain, and recites a prayer of invitation. Roll 2d6 plus Makoto. On a 10 or higher, the kami returns immediately — the shrine lights up with spiritual presence, the surrounding area begins to heal, and the Aruji establishes a new Kami Relationship at the Trusted level. On a 7 to 9, the kami stirs but does not fully return — the shrine is functional at the hokora level, providing basic services, but full jinja-level restoration requires additional work: more offerings, a matsuri celebration, or the resolution of whatever drove the kami away in the first place. On a 6 or less, the kami does not respond.
