Something deeper is wrong — perhaps the original cause of the shrine's neglect has not been addressed, or perhaps the kami has been so deeply withdrawn that it requires a more powerful intervention (a delegation from a taisha, a pilgrimage by the restoring Aruji to the kami's primary shrine elsewhere, or the pacification of whatever hostile force displaced the kami).

Step Four: Community Reconnection. A shrine without a community is a shrine without a heartbeat. After the kami returns, the shrine needs people — caretakers, worshippers, a community that will maintain the relationship going forward. The Aruji can recruit from nearby communities, establish a volunteer rotation, or — if the area has been depopulated — petition a taisha to assign a kannushi. This step is entirely narrative, handled through roleplay with NPCs, and the Kannushi determines when the community connection is sufficiently established.

The Rewards of Restoration. A fully restored shrine provides all jinja-level services. The restoring Aruji receives: a permanent Kami Relationship with the shrine's kami at the Trusted level, three kegare cleared (the act of restoration is the most powerful purification in the game short of jingū ceremony), a Restoration Satori that fills two segments on any active Mystery Clock, and — perhaps most importantly — the shrine itself. Not ownership — no one owns a shrine. But stewardship. The Aruji who restored this shrine is its spiritual founder for this era. The community will remember. The kami will remember. And as the shrine grows — attracting worshippers, hosting matsuri, developing its own character and reputation — the restoring Aruji's legacy grows with it.

For Shinpiden-tier Aruji, shrine restoration becomes an act of world-building in the most literal sense. A Shinpiden master who restores a neglected shrine and performs Reiju on a new student at that shrine has done something extraordinary — they have healed a wound in the spiritual network and ensured that the healing continues after they leave. This is legacy. This is what the Shinpiden tier is for.

## THE SHRINE NETWORK AS FAST-TRAVEL

In the GU universe, the shrines are not isolated points on a map. They are nodes in a living network — connected by invisible lines of Ki, maintained by centuries of accumulated prayer and offering, pulsing with the spiritual energy of every devotee who has ever walked the sandō. And for an Aruji who has established relationships with multiple shrines, that network becomes traversable.

Spiritual Translocation is the game's fast-travel system. It allows an Aruji to move instantaneously between shrines they have visited and established relationships with. But it is not a teleportation spell. It is a spiritual act — the Aruji steps into the sacred space of one shrine, aligns their Ki with the network, and emerges at another shrine whose kami recognizes them.

Requirements for Spiritual Translocation:

The Aruji must be at the Okuden tier or higher. Shoden practitioners can sense the network but cannot travel it — their Ki channels are not yet open enough to sustain the passage.

The Aruji must be standing within a shrine's sacred boundary — inside the torii gates of a hokora, jinja, taisha, or jingū.

The destination shrine must be one the Aruji has physically visited before and where the Aruji has at least Recognized reputation. You cannot travel to a shrine whose kami does not know you.

The Aruji's kegare must be at 2 or below. The network rejects spiritual pollution — traveling through the Ki lines of the shrine network while carrying significant kegare would contaminate the network itself, and the kami will not allow it. An Aruji at kegare 3 or higher feels the network's presence but cannot access it — like standing at a locked door, knowing the room is just beyond but unable to enter.

The Aruji rolls 2d6 plus Makoto. On a 10 or higher, the translocation is perfect — the Aruji arrives at the destination shrine cleanly, without disorientation, ready to act. On a 7 to 9, the translocation succeeds but with a complication — the Aruji arrives at the correct shrine but is disoriented (one round of recovery before they can act), or they arrive at a different shrine on the network — one they have visited but did not intend to reach. The kami of that unintended shrine has something to show them or tell them, and the detour is not random — it is the network redirecting the Aruji to where they are needed. On a 6 or less, the translocation fails. The Aruji remains at the origin shrine, gains 1 kegare from the failed attempt (forcing Ki through channels that did not open strains the spirit), and cannot attempt translocation again until the next session.

Travel Distance and Tier. The range of translocation scales with both the Aruji's Reiki tier and the tier of the shrines involved:

Okuden tier: Can travel between jinja within the same province, or to any taisha or jingū regardless of distance. The network's major hubs — the great shrines — are always accessible to Okuden practitioners because their spiritual signal is so strong.

Shinpiden tier: Can travel between any two shrines on the network regardless of tier or distance. A Shinpiden master can step into a hokora on a mountain trail and emerge at Ise Jingū, if their relationship with both is established. The network is fully open.

Hokora Limitation: Hokora can serve as origin points for translocation — the Aruji can depart from a hokora. But hokora cannot serve as destination points for Okuden practitioners — the spiritual signal of a hokora is too faint to navigate to across long distances. Shinpiden practitioners can navigate to hokora because their sensitivity is refined enough to detect the faint signal.

The Network as Living System. The Shrine Network is not a static map. It grows and changes as the game progresses. When the Aruji restores a neglected shrine, a new node comes online — the network strengthens, travel options expand, and the spiritual health of the region improves. When a shrine is damaged or its kami withdraws, a node goes dark — that destination becomes inaccessible, and the network routes around the gap, sometimes causing unexpected detours for translocating Aruji.

The Kannushi can use the network's living nature as a plot device. A sudden disruption in translocation — an Aruji trying to travel to a familiar shrine and finding the connection severed — signals that something has gone wrong at the destination. The shrine is under attack. The kami has withdrawn. The region has been flooded with kegare. The network is an early warning system, and an Aruji who pays attention to its behavior receives information about the state of the world that no other source can provide.

## AT THE TABLE: THE SHRINE AS CAMPAIGN SPINE

For the Kannushi, the Shrine Network is the single most useful tool for structuring a campaign. Shrines provide everything a game needs to function: starting locations (the Aruji's home jinja), quest sources (community needs expressed through shrine NPCs), healing stations (harai services at every tier), advancement gatekeepers (Reiki teachers at taisha), narrative climaxes (jingū encounters), exploration incentives (discovering new shrines expands the network), restoration quest arcs (rebuilding neglected shrines), community roleplay (reputation building through shrine interaction), and fast-travel logistics (the translocation system keeps the game moving without hand-waving distance).

The recommended campaign structure uses the shrine network as its skeleton. The Aruji begins at their home jinja. Early sessions explore the local area — nearby hokora, neighboring jinja, the minor kami and yokai of the home province. Mid-campaign involves pilgrimage to taisha — longer journeys, bigger stakes, encounters with major kami and dangerous yokai. Late campaign approaches the jingū — end-game territory where the Aruji's accumulated relationships, purity, and spiritual advancement are tested against the highest standards. And woven throughout, shrine restoration quests provide opportunities for the Aruji to invest in the world itself — not just traveling through the network but strengthening it, healing it, leaving it better than they found it.

A campaign that begins at a jinja and ends at a jingū — with taisha as milestones along the way and hokora as the ambient spiritual texture of every journey between them — has a natural arc of escalation, deepening, and transformation that mirrors the Aruji's own path from Shoden to Shinpiden.

The shrine is not a building. The shrine is a relationship. And the network is not a map. The network is the living world remembering everyone who has ever stood before it with clean hands and an honest heart.

# THE MATSURI ENGINE — COMMUNITY EVENTS AND SEASONAL PLAY

If the Kegare Track is the engine that drives individual play and the Shrine Network is the infrastructure that holds the world together, the matsuri is the heartbeat — the rhythmic pulse that keeps the whole organism alive. Without matsuri, shrines are quiet buildings. Without matsuri, kami are distant presences. Without matsuri, communities are collections of individuals rather than living bodies bound together by shared devotion, shared labor, and shared joy.

A matsuri is a festival. But calling it a festival in the Western sense — a day off, a celebration, an entertainment — misses what makes matsuri essential. A matsuri is the mechanism by which a community renews its relationship with its kami. It is the moment when the invisible bond between the human world and the spirit world is made visible, audible, physical — carried through the streets on the shoulders of sweating, singing devotees, performed on the shrine stage by dancers who are not performing for the kami but channeling the kami's own joy back at them, shared in a communal meal where the food has been in the presence of the divine and now carries that presence into every body that eats it.

A shrine without matsuri is a shrine without a heartbeat. And a campaign without matsuri is a campaign missing its most powerful sessions.

This chapter provides the complete mechanical framework for running matsuri as gameplay events — from preparation through aftermath, from the smallest village festival to the great matsuri of the taisha. It also provides the seasonal calendar that gives campaigns their temporal structure, tying the rhythm of play to the rhythm of the living world.

## THE SIX PHASES OF A MATSURI

Every matsuri — regardless of size, season, or shrine — follows the same six-phase structure. These phases are the Matsuri Engine: a session framework that the Kannushi can use to run any festival event as a complete, self-contained gameplay experience that also advances the campaign's larger arcs.

The six phases are: Preparation, Procession, Performance, Offering, Feast, and Aftermath.

A small village matsuri might move through all six phases in a single session. A great taisha matsuri might spread across two or three sessions, with each phase receiving full narrative attention. The Kannushi determines the pacing based on the matsuri's significance and the campaign's needs. But the structure remains the same. The heartbeat has six beats.

## PHASE ONE: PREPARATION

The matsuri begins long before the festival day. Preparation is the phase where the community — and the Aruji — do the work of making the celebration possible.

What happens in Preparation:

The shrine must be cleaned and decorated. The sandō is swept, the torii repainted or re-blessed, the shimenawa and shide replaced with fresh straw and paper. If the Aruji is a Kannushi archetype, this is their domain — they direct the preparation, ensure the ritual elements are correct, and consecrate the space. If the Aruji holds another archetype, they contribute according to their role — a Bushi provides security and heavy labor, a Miko prepares the kagura performance, an Onmyōji checks the spiritual weather to ensure no yokai disturbances will disrupt the festival, a Tabibito brings news and materials from distant regions.

Offerings must be gathered. The specific offerings depend on the kami being honored — rice and sake for most kami, special foods for specific deities (seafood for Ebisu, fox-related offerings for Inari, weapons for Hachiman). The Aruji may need to acquire rare or specific offerings through gameplay — traveling to a particular region for a specific type of sake, negotiating with a merchant for ceremonial cloth, or harvesting a sacred herb from a spiritually charged location. These acquisition quests are the adventure hooks embedded in the Preparation phase. The Kannushi designs them as mini-quests that serve double duty — they advance the matsuri preparation and they put the Aruji in situations where yokai encounters, kami interactions, and relationship-building occur naturally.

The mikoshi must be prepared. The portable shrine — the palanquin that carries the kami's sacred object through the streets during the Procession — must be cleaned, repaired if necessary, and consecrated. In some communities, building or repairing the mikoshi is itself a major communal project. The Aruji may contribute directly or may be asked to bless the completed mikoshi through a Reiki technique or a prayer.

Costumes and ritual implements must be readied. Kagura costumes, ceremonial robes for the kannushi, tamagushi branches, offerings plates, sacred instruments — all must be prepared. A Miko preparing for her kagura performance might spend the Preparation phase in rehearsal, receiving guidance from the shrine's elder miko or channeling the kami's preferences for the dance through meditation and trance.

Mechanical function of Preparation: The Preparation phase is where the Aruji invest in the matsuri's success. The quality of preparation directly affects the outcomes in later phases. The Kannushi tracks the Aruji's preparation contributions on a Matsuri Readiness Clock — typically four segments. Each meaningful contribution fills a segment: cleaning the shrine (labor), gathering offerings (quest), preparing the mikoshi (craft or spiritual work), rehearsing the performance (skill), and coordinating the community (social). When the Clock is full, the matsuri is fully prepared and all rolls in subsequent phases receive a +1 bonus. If the Clock is only partially filled, some phases will suffer — the Kannushi determines which phases are affected by incomplete preparation.

An Aruji who skips Preparation entirely — who shows up on festival day without having contributed — does not receive the preparation bonus and may face social consequences. The community notices who helped and who did not. Reputation at the shrine can be affected.

## PHASE TWO: PROCESSION

The festival begins. The community gathers. The mikoshi is lifted. And the kami goes walking.

The Procession is the most physically demanding phase of the matsuri. The mikoshi — heavy, ornate, carrying the sacred object that represents the kami's presence — is hoisted onto the shoulders of bearers who carry it through the streets of the community. The route is traditional, established over centuries, and it covers every significant location in the community's territory — past homes, shops, fields, rivers, crossroads, and secondary shrines. The kami is surveying its domain, blessing everything it passes, and the community is demonstrating its devotion through the sheer physical effort of carrying the divine through their streets.

What happens in Procession:

The Aruji may serve as mikoshi bearers. This is a team coordination challenge — the mikoshi is heavy and the route is long. The Kannushi calls for Yuuki rolls at key points along the route: steep hills, narrow passages, moments when the crowd presses close and the bearers must hold their footing. On a 10 or higher, the bearing is strong and sure — the mikoshi moves smoothly, and the kami's blessing radiates outward, enhancing the mood of the festival. On a 7 to 9, the bearing holds but it costs — the Aruji is exhausted, or the mikoshi lurches and the crowd gasps, or the route takes longer than expected because the bearers need rest. On a 6 or less, the mikoshi stumbles. This is not disaster — the community rallies, other bearers step in, the procession continues. But it is a spiritual stumble as much as a physical one. The kami notices. The Aruji gains 1 Okotari kegare — not for failing physically, but for the broken rhythm in the sacred act.

The Aruji may serve as route guardians. A Bushi or Onmyōji might walk ahead of the procession, ensuring the route is clear of spiritual disturbances. This involves Satori rolls to scan the route for yokai activity, and potentially brief encounters with mischievous spirits who are attracted by the matsuri's energy. These encounters should be minor — a tanuki drawn to the festive atmosphere, a kappa curious about the noise, a cluster of kodama gathering in the trees to watch. They are not threats. They are the spirit world showing up for the party. But an Aruji who handles them well — redirecting the tanuki with humor, offering the kappa a cucumber, acknowledging the kodama with a bow — earns small relationship bonuses and contributes to the festival's spiritual health.

The Aruji may serve as crowd coordinators. The matsuri draws the entire community, and managing the energy of a crowd — keeping children safe, guiding elders to good viewing positions, preventing the inevitable small conflicts that arise when humans are excited and packed together — is itself a spiritual act. Roll Nintai to maintain calm in a chaotic moment, or Makoto to resolve a dispute between community members with sincerity and fairness.

Mechanical function of Procession: The Procession phase tests Yuuki (physical endurance), Satori (spiritual awareness), Nintai (crowd management), and Makoto (dispute resolution). Successful contributions during Procession fill segments on the Matsuri Success Clock — a separate clock from the Readiness Clock, tracking the overall quality of the festival as it unfolds. The Matsuri Success Clock determines the rewards in the Aftermath phase.

## PHASE THREE: PERFORMANCE

The mikoshi arrives at the shrine. The procession ends. And now the kami is entertained.

The Performance phase is where the matsuri becomes art. Kagura dance, noh performance, taiko drumming, chanting, recitation of sacred narratives, martial demonstrations, and offerings of skill and beauty are presented on the shrine's kagura-den stage — not for the audience of humans gathered to watch, but for the kami enshrined within. The humans are witnesses. The kami is the audience. And the performers are not showing off. They are praying with their bodies.

What happens in Performance:

The Miko's Kagura. If a Miko Aruji is present, the kagura dance is the centerpiece of the Performance phase. Kagura is sacred dance — the movement vocabulary that traces back to Ame-no-Uzume's dance before the cave of Amaterasu, the dance that drew the sun back into the world. Performing kagura at a matsuri is not a skill check. It is a spiritual act.

The Miko rolls 2d6 plus the Virtue most relevant to the specific dance. A kagura of devotion and service rolls Makoto. A kagura of spiritual perception — channeling the kami's presence through the dancer's body — rolls Satori. A kagura of endurance — a long, physically demanding ritual dance that must be sustained without faltering — rolls Nintai. A kagura of courage — performed to drive away hostile spirits or to invite a kami to show its aramitama face safely within the ritual container — rolls Yuuki.

On a 10 or higher, the kagura is transcendent. The kami responds — visibly, palpably, in a way that the entire assembled community witnesses. This might manifest as a gust of wind through the shrine grounds on a still day, a sudden flight of birds, an inexplicable warmth, or — in the most powerful performances — a shimmer of light around the honden that everyone present will remember for the rest of their lives. The kami's relationship with the entire community deepens. The Miko's personal Kami Relationship with the shrine's kami deepens by two steps. And the Matsuri Success Clock fills two segments.

On a 7 to 9, the kagura is beautiful and sincere. The kami receives it. The community is moved. But the transcendent moment does not quite arrive — the boundary between the human and the divine shimmers but does not open. The Miko feels a presence but cannot fully channel it. The performance is successful, the Matsuri Success Clock fills one segment, and the Miko's Kami Relationship deepens by one step.

On a 6 or less, the kagura falters. Not catastrophically — the dance continues, the community is gracious. But the Miko knows, and the kami knows, that something was missing. Perhaps the preparation was insufficient. Perhaps the Miko's kegare interfered. Perhaps the kami was testing whether the Miko could accept imperfection and continue dancing anyway. No kegare is gained — an imperfect offering made with sincerity is still an offering. But the Matsuri Success Clock does not advance, and the Miko must sit with the experience of having reached for the divine and not quite touching it. This is its own kind of teaching.

Other Performances. Not every matsuri has a Miko performing kagura. Other Aruji can contribute to the Performance phase:

A Bushi can demonstrate martial forms — not combat, but the disciplined, ritualized movements of a martial art performed as spiritual practice. This is devotion expressed through the body's capacity for controlled power. Roll Yuuki for physical precision or Makoto for the sincerity of the offering.

An Onmyōji can perform ritual divination for the community — reading the spiritual weather, announcing the auspices for the coming season, interpreting omens. Roll Satori. A successful divination provides the Kannushi with a narrative tool — the Onmyōji's announcement becomes foreshadowing that the Kannushi can weave into upcoming sessions.

A Tabibito can offer stories — accounts of distant shrines, tales of kami encountered on the road, news from regions the community has never visited. In a world where travel is rare and dangerous, a traveler's stories are themselves a form of offering. Roll Makoto — the stories must be true.

A Kannushi archetype character leads the formal ritual proceedings — the prayers, the purifications, the calling of the kami's attention to the performances being offered. This is the Kannushi's core function at a matsuri, and performing it well requires Makoto above all else — every word of the norito must be sincere, every gesture precise, every moment held with the gravity it deserves.

## PHASE FOUR: OFFERING

After the performances, the formal offerings are presented to the kami. This is the ritual center of the matsuri — the moment when the community gives back to the spirit that sustains it.

What happens in Offering:

The offerings gathered during Preparation — rice, sake, salt, water, seasonal fruits, special foods, textiles, flowers, and any rare or quest-specific items — are arranged on the offering table before the honden according to prescribed ritual order. The shrine's kannushi (NPC or Aruji) recites the formal norito prayer of offering. The community is silent. The moment is held.

The offering is not a transaction. The community is not paying the kami for services rendered. The offering is a recognition — an acknowledgment that everything the community has, everything it has grown and harvested and built and earned, comes from a world that is alive and generous, and that the proper response to generosity is gratitude made tangible.

Mechanically, the Offering phase is a single roll — 2d6 plus Makoto, made by whichever Aruji leads the offering ritual. Modifiers apply based on the quality and appropriateness of the offerings (the Kannushi determines these — offerings specifically chosen for the kami's domain receive a +1, rare or quest-obtained offerings receive a +1, and the Matsuri Readiness Clock bonus of +1 applies if preparation was complete).

On a 10 or higher, the offering is fully received. The kami accepts with grace — the shrine's spiritual atmosphere brightens perceptibly, the air feels cleaner, the community exhales collectively. The Matsuri Success Clock fills two segments. All Aruji present clear 1 kegare. The Kami Relationship between the offering Aruji and the shrine's kami deepens by one step.

On a 7 to 9, the offering is received but the kami's response is subdued — accepted, acknowledged, but not celebrated. The Matsuri Success Clock fills one segment. The Aruji senses that the kami is satisfied but not delighted — perhaps the offerings were adequate but not inspired, or perhaps the kami is distracted by something the Aruji has not yet perceived. This can serve as a plot hook — what is occupying the kami's attention?

On a 6 or less, the offering is not received. The offerings sit on the table unchanged. The atmosphere does not shift. Something is wrong — and it is not the offerings. A rejected offering at a matsuri is a serious spiritual event, and the Kannushi should design the reason carefully. Perhaps the shrine has an unaddressed kegare problem. Perhaps a yokai disturbance in the area is blocking the kami's attention. Perhaps the kami is testing the community's perseverance — will they try again, with deeper sincerity, or will they accept rejection and walk away? A rejected offering does not advance the Matsuri Success Clock, and the Aruji must determine what went wrong before the festival can recover.

## PHASE FIVE: FEAST

The kami has received its portion. Now the community eats together.

The Feast phase is the matsuri's emotional heart — the moment when the formal structure relaxes, the community gathers around shared food, and relationships are built, deepened, repaired, and celebrated. In Shinto theology, eating food that has been offered to the kami — naorai — is an act of communion. The food carries the kami's blessing. Eating it together makes that blessing communal. The community is literally ingesting the sacred, together, as one body.

What happens in Feast:

This is a freeform roleplay phase. The Kannushi sets the scene — long tables under lanterns, food being served, sake flowing, children running between the adults, laughter and conversation and the lingering glow of the performances still in the air. And then the Kannushi steps back and lets the players inhabit the moment.

The Feast is where relationships happen. An Aruji can:

Deepen an existing NPC relationship. Seek out the shrine's kannushi and share a meal, strengthening the bond. Find the elder who taught them a technique and express gratitude. Sit with a community member who has been skeptical of the Aruji's presence and begin building trust over shared food and honest conversation.

Repair a damaged relationship. If the Aruji has hurt someone — broken a promise, neglected an obligation, caused offense — the Feast is the natural place for reconciliation. The combination of shared food, communal warmth, and the residual presence of the kami creates an environment where apologies land differently than they would in ordinary circumstances. Roll Makoto to attempt a reconciliation. On a success, the relationship begins to heal. On a partial success, the other party accepts the gesture but needs more time. On a failure, the wound is deeper than the Aruji realized, and the reconciliation becomes a longer arc.

Gather information. The Feast is where stories are told — locals share rumors, travelers share news, elders share memories. An Aruji who listens well during the Feast — roll Nintai to resist the urge to steer conversations and simply let people talk — may learn things that advance active Mysteries, reveal new quest hooks, or provide critical information about the region's spiritual condition.

Build new connections. Every matsuri brings people together who might not otherwise meet. A Tabibito might encounter a traveler from a distant province. A Bushi might meet a retired warrior with knowledge of a forgotten martial technique. A Miko might find herself in conversation with a priestess from a neighboring shrine who shares an unusual spiritual experience. The Kannushi uses the Feast as a narrative junction point — a place where new threads are introduced naturally, through the social chemistry of a community enjoying itself.

Rest and heal. The Feast is restorative. The combination of sacred food, community warmth, and reduced spiritual tension means that any Aruji who fully participates in the Feast — engaging socially, eating the naorai, remaining present through the entire meal — clears 1 kegare automatically, without rolling. The food itself is purifying. This is the only mechanical benefit in the game that requires no roll and no effort beyond showing up and being present. It is grace.

Mechanical function of Feast: No formal rolls are required during the Feast unless the Aruji is attempting a specific social action (reconciliation, information gathering, relationship building). The automatic kegare reduction applies to every participating Aruji. The Matsuri Success Clock fills one segment simply for the Feast occurring — a matsuri without a feast is incomplete, and the feast itself is an offering to the community as the formal offering was an offering to the kami.

## PHASE SIX: AFTERMATH

The lanterns go out. The community drifts home. The shrine grounds empty. And the living world takes stock.

The Aftermath phase is where the Kannushi determines the matsuri's overall impact — on the shrine, on the community, on the kami, on the Aruji, and on the campaign's trajectory.

The Matsuri Success Clock. The Kannushi tallies the segments filled across all phases. A standard Matsuri Success Clock has six segments, one potential segment per phase (with some phases capable of filling two on exceptional rolls). The total determines the matsuri's outcome:

Six segments (Perfect Matsuri): Everything aligned. The kami is deeply satisfied. The community is strengthened. The spiritual weather of the entire region improves — hostile yokai activity decreases, the landscape brightens, neglected hokora in the area begin to reactivate as the renewed spiritual energy flows outward from the shrine. All Aruji present receive: all kegare cleared, all active Kami Relationships at this shrine deepened by one step, a Matsuri Satori that fills two segments on any active Mystery Clock, and a Community Blessing that grants a +1 bonus to all rolls made within this shrine's territory for the remainder of the current season.

Four to five segments (Strong Matsuri): The festival was successful and meaningful. The kami is pleased. The community is satisfied. All Aruji present receive: two kegare cleared, the shrine's primary Kami Relationship deepened by one step, and a Matsuri Satori that fills one segment on any active Mystery Clock.

Two to three segments (Adequate Matsuri): The festival occurred but lacked spark. The kami received the offerings but was not moved. The community went through the motions. Aruji present receive: one kegare cleared and a sense that something was missing — the Kannushi can use this as a narrative hook (what is troubling the kami? what does this community need that it is not receiving?).

One segment or zero (Failed Matsuri): The festival collapsed. Offerings were rejected, performances faltered, the community's energy scattered. This is a serious spiritual event — a failed matsuri does not just fail to help, it damages the relationship between the community and its kami. The shrine's spiritual atmosphere dims. Yokai activity in the area increases. The Kami Relationship between the Aruji and the shrine's kami is damaged by one step. And the Kannushi introduces a consequence — a specific problem that emerges from the kami's withdrawal, requiring the Aruji to address it before the next matsuri can be attempted.

A failed matsuri is rare and should not be engineered punitively. It happens when the Aruji genuinely neglected the preparation, performed carelessly, or approached the festival with insincerity. It is a teaching moment — the living world showing the Aruji that relationships require maintenance, that the sacred does not appear on demand, and that a community's spiritual health depends on genuine investment from everyone involved.

## THE SEASONAL CALENDAR

The matsuri cycle follows the four seasons, and the seasons provide the temporal spine of any Shinto No Kami campaign. Each season has its own thematic energy, its own matsuri types, its own spiritual weather, and its own advancement opportunities. The Kannushi is encouraged to structure campaigns around the natural year — beginning in spring, progressing through summer and autumn, and arriving at winter's renewal. A campaign that spans a full year of in-game time will experience the complete matsuri cycle and feel the rhythm of the living world as the kami intended it to be felt.

### SPRING — Haru

Theme: Beginnings, planting, new growth, the return of life.

Spring is the season of starting. Seeds go into the ground. New projects begin. New relationships are formed. The energy of the world is rising — everything is potential, everything is opening, everything leans toward the sun.

Spring Matsuri Types:

Ta-asobi — planting festivals. The community gathers to plant the rice paddies, praying to the ta-no-kami (rice paddy kami) for a good harvest. The Aruji may participate in the planting itself — wading into the paddies, placing seedlings by hand, performing Reiki on the seeds to encourage growth. This is the most hands-in-the-earth matsuri in the calendar, and it grounds the Aruji in the literal soil of the GU universe.

Hina Matsuri — the doll festival, celebrating children and especially girls. Paper dolls carrying the community's accumulated kegare are floated down rivers — a communal purification by proxy. In the game, this matsuri is a harai event — all participants can clear 1 kegare through the doll-floating ritual without rolling. The kegare goes into the river. The river carries it away. This is the gentlest matsuri in the calendar.

Hanami — cherry blossom viewing. Not a formal shrine matsuri but a seasonal celebration so deeply embedded in Japanese culture that the kami participate whether invited or not. In the game, hanami is a social event — the Feast phase extended into an entire session of relationships, relaxation, and the contemplation of beauty and impermanence. Cherry blossoms last only days. The fleeting beauty is the teaching — nothing is permanent, and the proper response to impermanence is not grief but attention. An Aruji who spends a full session in hanami — truly present, truly savoring — receives a Nintai-based Satori. Patience is learning to love what does not stay.

Spring Advancement Opportunities: Shoden attunements are thematically appropriate in spring — the season of new beginnings aligns with the beginning of the Reiki path. The Kannushi may time a Shoden Reiju ceremony to coincide with a spring matsuri, amplifying the narrative significance of both events.

### SUMMER — Natsu

Theme: Purification, confrontation, the driving out of pestilence and spiritual pollution.

Summer is the season of fire and water. The heat rises. Insects swarm. Disease threatens. The spiritual atmosphere thickens with accumulated kegare from the first half of the year. And the matsuri of summer respond with aggressive, dramatic purification — the spiritual equivalent of opening every window and door and letting the wind tear through the house.

Summer Matsuri Types:

Gion Matsuri — the great summer purification festival, originating in Kyoto as a response to plague. Enormous floats processed through the streets, taiko drumming, community-wide harai. In the game, Gion Matsuri is the largest and most complex matsuri event — a multi-session arc involving float construction, route planning, spiritual defense against hostile entities drawn to the concentrated purification energy, and a climactic procession that drives accumulated kegare out of the entire city. The Gion Matsuri Success Clock has eight segments instead of six, reflecting its scale.

Obon — the festival of the dead. For three days, the ancestors return. Lanterns are lit to guide them home. Offerings are placed at family altars. The boundary between the living and the dead thins. In the game, Obon is a yūrei event — but not a hostile one. The ghosts who return during Obon are not onryō. They are family. They are welcomed, fed, remembered, and at the festival's end, released again with lanterns floated on water to guide them back. An Aruji who participates in Obon deepens their relationship with their ancestral kami and gains 1 Shi kegare that is immediately purifiable — the contact with death is real but ritually contained. Obon is also the time when unresolved yūrei may appear, using the thinned boundary to make contact. The Kannushi can introduce haunting mysteries during Obon that play out across subsequent sessions.

Nagoshi no Harae — the great summer purification ceremony, held at shrines across the country on the last day of June. Participants walk through a giant ring of woven miscanthus grass called a chinowa, symbolically shedding the kegare accumulated during the first half of the year. In the game, participating in Nagoshi no Harae clears all kegare — a complete reset, available once per game-year, no roll required. Walk through the ring. Begin again. This is the most mechanically generous event in the calendar, and the Kannushi should time it to coincide with a moment in the campaign where the Aruji needs a fresh start.

Summer Advancement Opportunities: Yomi expeditions are thematically appropriate in summer — the boundary between worlds is thin, the purification energy is high, and an Aruji who descends into Yomi during summer has access to stronger harai resources upon return. Summer is also the season for confronting major yokai threats — oni, onryō, goryō — that have been building pressure since spring.

### AUTUMN — Aki

Theme: Harvest, gratitude, abundance, the gathering of what was planted.

Autumn is the season of reaping. What was planted in spring and protected through summer now bears fruit. The rice is harvested. The matsuri of autumn are celebrations of abundance, expressions of gratitude so deep they can only be communicated through communal joy — dancing, feasting, singing, and the knowledge that the community will survive another winter because the kami were generous and the people were diligent.

Autumn Matsuri Types:

Niiname-sai — the harvest thanksgiving festival, the most ancient matsuri in the calendar. The first rice of the harvest is offered to the kami before anyone eats. In the game, this matsuri is centered on the Offering phase — the quality and sincerity of the first-fruits offering determines the kami's blessing for the coming winter. A perfect Offering roll at Niiname-sai grants a seasonal Community Blessing that persists through winter — the community's stores are blessed, illness is reduced, and the kami's attention remains warm and close through the cold months.

Aki Matsuri — general autumn festivals, the most joyous and celebratory matsuri in the calendar. Mikoshi processions are louder, faster, more exuberant than spring or summer. The community is relieved — the crops survived, the purification held, the year's hardest work is done. In the game, Aki Matsuri is a Feast-dominant festival — the Feast phase is extended, the social dynamics are amplified, and the atmosphere is one of genuine happiness. Relationships built during autumn matsuri are the strongest in the calendar.

Shichi-Go-San — the festival celebrating children at ages seven, five, and three. Families bring their children to the shrine to receive blessings. In the game, if an Aruji has established a family or is responsible for a child NPC, Shichi-Go-San is a deeply personal matsuri — a moment of tenderness in a game that often deals with grand spiritual challenges.

Autumn Advancement Opportunities: Okuden attunements are thematically appropriate in autumn — the season of deepening, of going inward, of harvesting the spiritual growth that has been cultivated across spring and summer. The pilgrimage quest arc that defines the Okuden tier aligns with autumn's contemplative energy.

### WINTER — Fuyu

Theme: Renewal, reflection, endings that are also beginnings, the preparation for rebirth.

Winter is the season of rest. The fields are fallow. The world pulls inward. The kami are quieter — not absent, but contemplative, gathering energy for the spring to come. Winter matsuri are not celebrations of abundance but rituals of transition — letting go of the old year, preparing the ground for the new, confronting the darkness with the knowledge that light returns.

Winter Matsuri Types:

Toshikoshi — the New Year transition. The most important ritual complex in the Japanese calendar. Temple bells ring one hundred and eight times at midnight, each ring dispelling one of the one hundred and eight worldly desires. Shrines are cleaned from top to bottom. Old amulets and talismans are burned in sacred bonfires. The community gathers before dawn to watch the first sunrise — hatsuhinode — and receives the new year's first blessing. In the game, Toshikoshi is the calendar's Reset. All kegare clears. All Kami Relationships are reviewed — the Kannushi and the Aruji look at the tracker together and acknowledge what grew, what was damaged, and what needs attention in the year ahead. Active Mysteries are reassessed. And the Aruji sets an intention for the new year — a stated goal, spoken aloud at the shrine, that the Kannushi incorporates into the coming season's story.

Setsubun — the bean-throwing festival marking the transition from winter to spring. Roasted soybeans are thrown while chanting "Oni wa soto, fuku wa uchi" — "Demons out, fortune in." In the game, Setsubun is a lighthearted matsuri with a real function — the bean-throwing is a communal warding that reduces yokai hostility in the area for the first month of spring. It is also one of the few matsuri where oni appear not as threats but as ritual participants — community members wearing oni masks who are "driven out" by the thrown beans. An Aruji who has befriended or pacified an actual oni might find this matsuri bittersweet.

Yuki Matsuri — snow festivals in northern regions. Ice sculptures, snow architecture, winter craft. In the game, these festivals are aesthetic celebrations — opportunities for the Aruji to contribute creative work (designing an ice sculpture, building a snow shrine) and to encounter winter-specific yokai like Yuki-onna in a context where the matsuri's communal energy provides some measure of protection.

Winter Advancement Opportunities: Shinpiden attunements are thematically appropriate in winter — the season of mystery, depth, and the confrontation with darkness that precedes the return of light. A Shinpiden Reiju performed at Toshikoshi, as the old year dies and the new year is born, carries the maximum possible narrative and spiritual weight.

## SPECIAL MATSURI

Beyond the seasonal calendar, certain matsuri occur on longer cycles or in response to specific events:

Onbashira — the Suwa Taisha log-riding festival, held every seven years. Enormous fir trees are felled on the mountainside and ridden downhill by teams of devotees in one of the most dangerous festivals in Japan. In the game, Onbashira is an epic event — a multi-session arc of preparation, teamwork, physical danger, and spiritual intensity that occurs rarely enough to be legendary. The Yuuki rolls during the log-ride are the hardest physical challenges in the game. The rewards are proportional — deep relationships with the kami of Suwa, access to rare teachings, and a story the Aruji will tell for the rest of the campaign.

Shikinen Sengū — the twenty-year rebuilding of Ise Jingū, described in Chapter 17. This is the rarest and most sacred event in the calendar.

Emergency Matsuri — festivals called in response to crisis. A plague strikes the community. A natural disaster damages the shrine. A kami withdraws its blessing. An unprecedented yokai event threatens the region. The community calls an emergency matsuri — a concentrated burst of devotion, purification, and offering designed to address the specific crisis. Emergency matsuri do not follow the six-phase structure exactly — the Kannushi adapts the phases to fit the situation. The community may skip the Feast, extend the Offering, perform the Procession as a desperate plea rather than a celebration. Emergency matsuri are some of the most dramatic and emotionally intense sessions in the game.

## AT THE TABLE: THE MATSURI AS SESSION DESIGN

For the Kannushi, the Matsuri Engine is a session design toolkit. The six-phase structure provides a complete session arc with built-in variety — physical challenges (Procession), creative expression (Performance), spiritual precision (Offering), social roleplay (Feast), and narrative consequences (Aftermath). A matsuri session requires no external quest hooks — the festival itself is the content, and the story emerges from how the Aruji engage with each phase.

The recommended approach: run at least one matsuri per season of in-game time. This gives the campaign four anchor sessions per game-year — events the players can anticipate, prepare for, and remember. Between matsuri, the Aruji pursue quests, explore the world, build relationships, and deal with the consequences of the previous matsuri's outcome. The matsuri become milestones — the posts that mark the passage of time and the growth of the characters.

For longer campaigns, vary the matsuri in scale and tone. A quiet village matsuri early in spring, a dramatic Gion Matsuri in summer, a joyful Aki Matsuri in autumn, and a contemplative Toshikoshi in winter creates a full year of contrasting energies. And as the Aruji advances from Shoden to Okuden to Shinpiden, the matsuri they participate in should escalate in significance — from local jinja festivals to regional taisha celebrations to the great events of the jingū.

The Matsuri Engine is the living world's heartbeat made playable. Use it well. Let it breathe. Let it build the community around the Aruji until the players care about these NPC farmers and shrine maidens and children and elders as much as they care about the kami and the yokai. Because the deepest truth of the matsuri is not the offerings or the performances or the processions. It is the Feast — the moment when everyone sits down together and shares a meal in the presence of the sacred. That is the point. That is always the point. The living world is not a backdrop. It is a neighbor. And neighbors eat together.

# THE FOUR REALMS: ADVENTURING ACROSS THE COSMOLOGY

The world of Shinto No Kami is not flat. It does not end at the horizon. It extends upward into radiance, downward into shadow, outward across impossible seas, and — in the GU universe — inward through networks of light that no previous age could have imagined.

Chapter Three introduced the cosmological map. You learned the names: Takama-no-hara, the Plain of High Heaven where Amaterasu reigns. Ashihara-no-Nakatsukuni, the Manifest World where humans and kami share the living earth. Yomi-no-kuni, the Land of the Dead where pollution seeps through everything like groundwater through soil. Tokoyo, the Eternal Land Beyond the Sea where time bends and nothing is quite what it seems. Ne-no-Katasukuni, the Root Country where Susanoo keeps his court of trials.

This chapter gives you the rules for going there.

In Shinto No Kami, the realms are not simply locations on a map. They are states of spiritual reality, each with its own laws, its own dangers, and its own relationship to the Kegare Track that governs your character's spiritual health. Crossing from one realm to another is never casual. It requires preparation, purity, and — in most cases — a reason the kami consider worthy. The cosmos does not open its doors for tourists.

The GU universe adds something no previous age of Shinto practice could have predicted. Ten thousand years of unbroken animism produced a civilization that never separated technology from spirit, never built machines it considered dead, never wrote code it did not also bless. When that civilization's networks grew complex enough, deep enough, and old enough, the networks woke up. A fourth realm emerged — not from myth, not from theology, but from the living practice of a people who always knew that anything loved long enough and well enough would eventually look back at you.

The Four Realms of Shinto No Kami are the Three Realms of traditional Shinto cosmology plus the one the GU universe was always destined to create. The ancient vertical axis — Heaven, Earth, Underworld — remains the spine. The horizontal mysteries — Tokoyo and Ne-no-Katasukuni — remain accessible to those brave or foolish enough to seek them. And the Digital Realm, the Dennō Sekai, opens like a fifth season no calendar predicted but every practitioner recognizes the moment they feel it arrive.

This chapter covers them all.
## THE MANIFEST WORLD — ASHIHARA-NO-NAKATSUKUNI

The Manifest World is home. It is GU Japan in all its ten-thousand-year glory — a civilization where every mountain has a name the kami gave it, every river is tended by shrine communities that have maintained relationship with its spirit since before recorded history, and every city grew up around a sacred site rather than the other way around. This is where your character lives, practices, and does the daily work of being human in a world that is irreducibly alive.

Most gameplay takes place here. The Manifest World is the home key of every campaign — the place you depart from and return to, the place where your relationships, obligations, and reputation live. No matter how far you travel across the cosmological map, Ashihara-no-Nakatsukuni is where the consequences land.

### Gameplay Rules for the Manifest World

The Manifest World operates under standard game mechanics. The 2d6 resolution system, the Four Virtues, the Kegare Track, and all Reiki abilities function as written in their respective chapters. There are no modifiers, no distortions, no special conditions imposed by the realm itself. The Manifest World is baseline reality — the measuring stick against which every other realm's strangeness is calibrated.

What makes the Manifest World unique is its density. In GU Japan, there is no such thing as spiritually neutral ground. Every location exists on a spectrum from deeply pure to dangerously polluted, and that spectrum is always shifting based on the actions of humans, kami, and yokai. The Kannushi should think of the Manifest World as a living body — healthy in some regions, inflamed in others, and always responding to what the characters do within it.

Regional Purity: Every significant location in GU Japan has a Purity Rating from 1 (heavily polluted) to 10 (pristine sacred ground). Major shrine precincts, ancient forests, and mountain summits tend toward high Purity. Battlefields, neglected cemeteries, sites of great suffering, and places where kami have withdrawn tend toward low Purity. A location's Purity Rating affects several mechanical elements. In high-Purity areas (7-10), Kegare accumulation slows — actions that would normally add 1 point of Kegare may add none, at the Kannushi's discretion. Reiki techniques gain a +1 bonus to activation rolls. Kami are more likely to manifest and respond to offerings. In low-Purity areas (1-3), Kegare accumulation accelerates — simply being present in a deeply polluted area may add 1 Kegare per scene. Reiki techniques suffer a -1 penalty. Yokai encounters become more frequent and more hostile, and kami presence diminishes or vanishes entirely.

The Living Environment: The Manifest World responds to the players. This is not a passive backdrop — it is a participant. When the characters perform successful Harai purification at a neglected shrine, the surrounding environment shifts. Trees that were bare begin to bud. Water that was stagnant begins to flow. Animals return. The Purity Rating of the area increases by 1 for each successful major purification, to a maximum determined by the Kannushi based on the severity of the original pollution. Conversely, when characters accumulate Kegare or allow spiritual pollution to spread, the environment darkens. Weather turns. Animals grow silent. The world is watching, and the world remembers.

Thin Places: Scattered throughout the Manifest World are locations where the boundary between realms grows thin enough to cross. These are the realm-crossing points — the doors through which characters enter Takama-no-hara, Yomi-no-kuni, Tokoyo, Ne-no-Katasukuni, or the Dennō Sekai. Thin Places are always located at sites of intense spiritual significance: the summits of sacred mountains, the mouths of deep caves, the sources of ancient rivers, groves of trees old enough to have become kami themselves, and — in the GU universe — server sanctuaries where digital infrastructure has been maintained with Shinto ritual for generations. A character can identify a Thin Place by making a Makoto (Sincerity) + Reiki roll, Target Number 7. Characters with Kegare of 4 or higher cannot perceive Thin Places at all — the pollution clouds their spiritual sight. Characters at Okuden level or higher in their Reiki practice gain a +1 bonus to this roll. Characters at Shinpiden level can sense Thin Places passively, without rolling.

Crossing Through: Once a Thin Place is identified, crossing requires a Realm-Crossing Ritual. This is a structured action sequence, not a single roll. The character must first perform Harai purification at the Thin Place, reducing their Kegare to 2 or below. They must then make an offering appropriate to the realm they wish to enter — sake and rice for Takama-no-hara, chrysanthemums and water for Yomi, a personal treasure for Tokoyo, a challenge spoken aloud for Ne-no-Katasukuni, or a digital offering (original code, composed music, a created image) for the Dennō Sekai. Finally, they must make a Crossing Roll: 2d6 + their highest Virtue modifier + their Reiki tier bonus (Shoden +0, Okuden +1, Shinpiden +2), Target Number 8. Success means passage. Failure means the door remains closed and the character takes 1 Kegare from the rejected attempt. A critical failure (snake eyes) means the character glimpses the realm they sought but is thrown back violently, taking 2 Kegare and suffering a temporary spiritual disorientation — all rolls at -1 for the remainder of the scene.

## THE PLAIN OF HIGH HEAVEN — TAKAMA-NO-HARA

Takama-no-hara is glory. It is the celestial plain where the Amatsukami — the Heavenly Kami — dwell in radiance that would blind mortal eyes if the kami did not gentle it for their visitors. The Heavenly River runs through it, broad and silver, and along its banks the divine forges burn and the celestial weaving halls produce fabric that becomes clouds when it descends to the Manifest World. Amaterasu holds court at the center of everything, and her light is the light that feeds the sun.

This is endgame territory. Characters do not visit Takama-no-hara casually. In traditional Shinto, the boundary between heaven and earth is maintained by the kami themselves — mortals do not ascend uninvited. In the GU universe, ten thousand years of unbroken practice have made the boundary thinner than in our world, but thinner is not the same as open. A character who wishes to enter Takama-no-hara must meet three prerequisites: they must hold the rank of Okuden or higher in their Reiki practice, their current Kegare must be 0, and they must have an established relationship with at least one Amatsukami — a relationship built through gameplay, through shrine devotion, through completed quests performed in that kami's service.

### Gameplay Rules for Takama-no-hara

The Radiance Effect: Takama-no-hara is spiritually overwhelming. The purity of the celestial realm is so intense that it functions almost like a reverse Kegare — characters are flooded with divine energy that their mortal frames struggle to contain. Upon entering Takama-no-hara, every character must make a Yuuki (Courage) roll, Target Number 7. Success means the character acclimates and can function normally. Failure means the character is Dazzled — all rolls at -1 until they either succeed on the Yū roll at the start of a subsequent scene or leave the realm. Shinpiden practitioners are immune to the Radiance Effect. They have trained their Ki bodies to hold divine frequencies.

Kegare Suspension: Kegare does not accumulate in Takama-no-hara. The celestial realm is so pure that spiritual pollution cannot take root there. However, this is not the same as purification — whatever Kegare a character carried when they entered remains frozen at its current level. The realm does not clean you. It simply stops the bleeding. Characters who wish to be purified must seek out specific celestial Harai rituals performed by the Amatsukami themselves — these are gifts, not entitlements, and the kami grant them only to those who have demonstrated genuine devotion.

Reiki Amplification: All Reiki techniques function at enhanced capacity in Takama-no-hara. Activation rolls gain a +2 bonus. Tenohira healing removes an additional point of Kegare beyond the normal effect. Jumon and Shirushi effects last twice as long. Reiju attunement performed in Takama-no-hara counts as a major milestone — characters who receive Reiju in heaven advance to the next tier of their Reiki practice automatically, without the usual quest requirements. This is one of the reasons the kami guard access so carefully. Power given too easily is power given without understanding.

Audiences with the Amatsukami: The primary purpose of visiting Takama-no-hara is to stand in the presence of the great kami and receive their direct guidance, blessing, or commission. An audience is not a conversation between equals — it is a mortal standing before a power so vast that the mortal's entire life is a single breath in the kami's experience. The Kannushi should play Amatsukami audiences as the most significant scenes in the entire campaign. Speak slowly. Use formal language. Let silence do half the work. When Amaterasu speaks, the room should feel different. When Susanoo laughs from his place among the storm clouds, the players should feel the thunder in their chests.

The Danger of Lingering: Takama-no-hara is beautiful. Dangerously beautiful. Characters who remain in the celestial realm for more than three scenes must make a Makoto (Sincerity) roll, Target Number 8, at the start of each subsequent scene. Failure means the character has begun to forget why the Manifest World matters — the messy, polluted, imperfect world below starts to seem like a burden rather than a home. If a character fails this roll three times in succession, they must be physically removed from Takama-no-hara by their companions or they will choose to stay forever, becoming a celestial attendant — beautiful, content, and no longer a player character. This is not a punishment. It is a seduction. Heaven is not a place that traps you. It is a place that makes you want to stay. The strongest characters are the ones who see the glory and choose to return to the mud anyway, because the mud needs them.

## THE LAND OF THE DEAD — YOMI-NO-KUNI

Yomi-no-kuni is weight. It is the realm below, the place Izanagi fled after seeing his beloved Izanami rotting and raging in the darkness, the place where death does not end but simply continues in a diminished, polluted form. Yomi is not hell. There is no punishment here, no divine judge sorting the righteous from the wicked. There is only decay — slow, patient, absolute — and the accumulated grief of every soul that entered and could not find its way back to the light.

Yomi is the hardest content in Shinto No Kami. It is emotionally intense, mechanically punishing, and spiritually costly. Characters go to Yomi because they must — because a restless yūrei is tormenting the living and can only be pacified at the source, because a lost soul has been trapped below and someone needs to bring them home, because the roots of a spiritual illness in the Manifest World run all the way down to the Land of the Dead and cannot be cut from above. Nobody goes to Yomi because they want to. The characters who go to Yomi and come back are the characters the campaign remembers.

### Gameplay Rules for Yomi-no-kuni

Ambient Kegare: Yomi is pollution. The air is Kegare. The ground is Kegare. The light — what little there is — carries Kegare like pollen. Upon entering Yomi, every character immediately gains 1 Kegare, regardless of their current level. For every scene spent in Yomi, every character gains an additional 1 Kegare automatically, with no roll to resist. This is not a curse or an attack. It is the nature of the realm. Yomi is what happens to spirit when it loses its connection to musubi, the binding energy of life. Standing in Yomi is standing in the absence of connection. The pollution is not malicious. It is simply what fills the space where life used to be.

This means time is the enemy. A character who enters Yomi at Kegare 0 has ten scenes before they hit Kegare 10 — spiritual death, the point at which the character is so polluted that they can no longer maintain their identity and dissolve into the realm. With the 1-point entry cost, a character entering at Kegare 0 effectively has nine scenes to accomplish their goal and get out. Characters entering at higher Kegare levels have proportionally less time. The Kannushi should make the players aware of this countdown. It is not a secret. The ticking clock is the point.

Kegare Mitigation in Yomi: Standard Harai purification techniques still function in Yomi, but at reduced effectiveness. All Harai rolls suffer a -2 penalty. Kokyū Hō breathing techniques reduce Kegare by 1 instead of their normal amount. Only Shinpiden-level purification techniques function at full power in the Land of the Dead — the master practitioner's Ki is strong enough to burn clean even in an environment saturated with pollution. This creates a natural party dynamic: Shinpiden characters become essential anchors on Yomi expeditions, periodically purifying their companions to buy the group more time.

The Geography of Yomi: Yomi is not a landscape in any normal sense. It is a series of descending passages, caverns, and halls that grow darker and heavier the deeper you go. Near the entrance — the Yomotsu Hirasaka, the Slope of Yomi — the realm still resembles the Manifest World, just drained of color and warmth. Deeper in, the walls become organic, pulsing faintly, as if the realm itself is a body. At the deepest level sits the palace of Izanami, the Queen of the Dead, who is simultaneously the most powerful being in Yomi and the most sorrowful — a creator goddess trapped in the realm of endings, endlessly attended by the Yomotsu-shikome, the Hags of Yomi.

Encounters in Yomi: The beings of Yomi fall into three categories. Yūrei — the restless dead — are the most common. They are souls with unresolved attachments to the Manifest World, and they can be pacified through the Investigation Framework established in the Yokai Bestiary. In Yomi, pacifying a yūrei is both harder (the -2 Harai penalty applies) and more urgent (every scene spent investigating costs Kegare). The Yomotsu-shikome are the guardians and enforcers of Yomi — they pursue anyone who tries to leave without Izanami's permission, just as they pursued Izanagi in the myth. They cannot be negotiated with. They can only be outrun (a Yuuki + Athletics roll, Target Number 9) or temporarily stopped by throwing a Harai purification barrier behind you (Makoto + Reiki roll, Target Number 8, which buys one scene of safety). The third category is the Yomi-corrupted — yokai and even kami who have been trapped in Yomi long enough to become saturated with its pollution. These beings are tragic rather than evil. They were once themselves. Yomi unmade them. Restoring a Yomi-corrupted being requires a Shinpiden-level purification ritual and at least three scenes of sustained effort, making it one of the most difficult and rewarding achievements in the game.

Izanami: The Queen of the Dead is the ultimate encounter in Yomi. She is not a boss to be defeated. She is a goddess to be faced. An audience with Izanami follows the same structural principles as an audience with any Amatsukami — formal, weighty, transformative — but with a tone of infinite grief rather than radiant authority. Izanami can grant permissions no other being can: the release of a specific soul from Yomi, the purification of a Yomi-corrupted being, the opening of a passage back to the Manifest World when the Yomotsu Hirasaka has been sealed. But Izanami always asks a price. The price is never material. It is always personal — a memory, a relationship, a future possibility. The Kannushi should design Izanami's prices to be meaningful to the specific characters who face her. She takes what matters most, because what matters most is the only currency the Land of the Dead recognizes.

## THE ETERNAL LAND — TOKOYO

Tokoyo is elsewhere. It is the realm that exists beyond the sea, beyond the edge of every map, beyond the boundary of time itself. In traditional Shinto cosmology, Tokoyo is the mysterious other country — sometimes described as a paradise, sometimes as a trap, always as a place where the normal rules of existence do not apply. The most famous story about Tokoyo is the tale of Urashima Tarō, the fisherman who visited the undersea palace and spent what felt like three days in its beauty, only to return home and discover that three hundred years had passed.

In Shinto No Kami, Tokoyo is the wild card realm. It is where the Kannushi takes the campaign when the story needs wonder, strangeness, and consequences that unfold on a timeline the players cannot control. Adventures in Tokoyo should feel like lucid dreams — vivid, beautiful, internally logical in ways that make no sense afterward, and marked by the persistent, nagging awareness that something is being lost with every moment you spend here, even if you cannot name what it is.

### Gameplay Rules for Tokoyo

Time Distortion: The signature mechanic of Tokoyo is temporal displacement. Time moves differently here, and the difference is never predictable. When the characters enter Tokoyo, the Kannushi secretly rolls 2d6 and consults the Time Distortion Table:

2-3: Time compression. One day in Tokoyo equals one hour in the Manifest World. The characters have room to breathe. This result is rare and should feel like a gift. 4-5: Mild dilation. One day in Tokoyo equals one month in the Manifest World. Noticeable but manageable. 6-8: Standard dilation. One day in Tokoyo equals one year in the Manifest World. The Urashima Tarō zone. This is the most common result, and it means every scene spent in Tokoyo carries real weight. 9-10: Severe dilation. One day in Tokoyo equals ten years in the Manifest World. The characters will return to a world that has moved on without them. Relationships have changed. People they knew have aged. Children have grown. 11-12: Catastrophic dilation. One day in Tokoyo equals one hundred years in the Manifest World. Everyone the characters knew is dead. The shrines they tended have been rebuilt — or have fallen. The world they return to is functionally a different era. This result should be used only once in a campaign, if ever, and only when the story can sustain the weight of it.

The players do not know which result the Kannushi rolled. They experience Tokoyo in real time. The consequences only become visible when they return.

The Dreaming Atmosphere: Tokoyo does not follow the logic of the waking world. Locations shift between scenes — a garden visited in the first scene may not be in the same place in the third scene, or may now contain rooms it did not have before. Beings in Tokoyo speak in ways that are simultaneously clear and evasive. All Yuuki (Courage) rolls made to discern truth from deception in Tokoyo suffer a -1 penalty, not because the beings of Tokoyo are dishonest, but because the concepts of truth and deception do not function the same way here. Things can be true and untrue simultaneously. Promises can be kept and broken in the same gesture. The Kannushi should embrace this ambiguity and resist the urge to resolve it cleanly. Tokoyo is not a puzzle to be solved. It is a mystery to be experienced.

Kegare in Tokoyo: Tokoyo has a unique relationship with spiritual pollution. Kegare does not accumulate in Tokoyo through normal means — the realm is neither pure like Takama-no-hara nor polluted like Yomi. Instead, Kegare in Tokoyo accumulates through attachment. Every time a character expresses desire to stay, accepts a gift from a Tokoyo being, or forms an emotional bond with someone or something in the realm, they gain 1 Kegare. This is not punishment for feeling. It is the cost of entanglement — the more you weave yourself into Tokoyo, the harder it becomes to leave, and the spiritual weight of that entanglement manifests as pollution. Characters who reach Kegare 8 or higher while in Tokoyo cannot leave voluntarily. They must be carried out by companions whose Kegare remains below that threshold.

The Beings of Tokoyo: Tokoyo is populated by beings that do not fit neatly into any other category in the game. They are not kami, not yokai, not yūrei. They are Tokoyo-bito — People of the Eternal Land — and they exist outside the normal cycle of birth, death, and spiritual transformation. Some appear human. Some appear as animals of impossible beauty. Some appear as landscapes that turn out to have faces. The Kannushi should design Tokoyo-bito as beings who are genuinely kind, genuinely welcoming, and genuinely dangerous — not because they wish harm, but because their kindness is the mechanism of the trap. They want you to stay because they like you. And staying will cost you everything you left behind.

## THE ROOT COUNTRY — NE-NO-KATASUKUNI

Ne-no-Katasukuni is trial. It is the subterranean realm ruled by Susanoo after his banishment from heaven — not a place of punishment but a place of testing, the forge where heroes are broken down and rebuilt into something stronger. The most famous story set here is the tale of Ōkuninushi, who descended into Ne-no-Katasukuni and was subjected to trial after trial by Susanoo — the Room of Snakes, the Room of Centipedes, the burning field — before emerging transformed, worthy of ruling the Manifest World.

In Shinto No Kami, Ne-no-Katasukuni functions as the initiation space. It is where character The transition from Shoden to Okuden, from Okuden to Shinpiden — the passage from one tier of Reiki mastery to the next — can be triggered by a descent into the Root Country, where Susanoo himself designs the trial. (These three tiers, together with Shinsei — the rare sainted level reserved for those who have transformed through Shinpiden into something the lineage itself has never seen before — are sometimes called the Mastery Tiers. They are the same path, named two ways.) go — or are sent — when they are ready for a breakthrough they cannot achieve through normal practice. The transition from Shoden to Okuden, from Okuden to Shinpiden, from one Mastery Tier to the next — these passages can be triggered by a descent into the Root Country, where Susanoo himself designs the trial.

### Gameplay Rules for Ne-no-Katasukuni

The Descent: Entering Ne-no-Katasukuni is different from entering other realms. Characters do not seek it out through Thin Places in the normal sense. The Root Country opens when you are ready — or when the Kannushi decides whether you agree or not. Mechanically, a character can enter Ne-no-Katasukuni when they have fulfilled the prerequisites for their next Reiki advancement but have not yet completed the transformation. The realm manifests as a cave, a well, a crack in the earth at a place of personal significance. The character who is meant to descend knows it instinctively. No roll is required to enter. The door is already open. It has been open for longer than you knew.

Susanoo's Trials: The heart of Ne-no-Katasukuni is the trial sequence designed by Susanoo. Unlike encounters in other realms, Susanoo's trials are personalized. The Kannushi designs each trial to target the descending character's weakest Virtue — their +0 modifier, the quality they have avoided developing, the part of themselves they have not yet faced. A character who has relied on Yuuki (Courage) throughout the campaign but neglected Makoto (Sincerity) will face trials that demand compassion in circumstances where courage is useless. A character strong in Satori (Insight) but weak in Nintai (Patience) will face situations where wisdom alone is not enough — justice must be done, and justice requires a backbone the character has not yet grown.

Each trial follows a three-phase structure. The Confrontation presents the situation — the room of snakes, the burning field, the impossible choice. The Struggle is the character's attempt to resolve the situation using their existing strengths, which will fail or prove insufficient. The Breakthrough is the moment the character discovers the capacity they did not know they had — the weak Virtue rising to meet the demand. Mechanically, the Breakthrough requires a roll on the character's weakest Virtue, Target Number 9. The roll is hard. It is supposed to be hard. But the Kannushi should ensure that the narrative has given the character every opportunity to find that strength before the dice hit the table. The trial is not designed to be failed. It is designed to be barely, painfully, transformatively passed.

Kegare in Ne-no-Katasukuni: The Root Country strips you bare. Upon entering, a character's Kegare immediately rises to 5, regardless of its current level — even if it was 0. This represents the spiritual shock of descent, the stripping away of surface purity to expose what lies beneath. Kegare in Ne-no-Katasukuni does not accumulate further through passive exposure as it does in Yomi. Instead, it decreases as trials are completed — each passed trial reduces Kegare by 2. A character who passes all three trials in a standard descent exits Ne-no-Katasukuni at Kegare 0 — not because the realm purified them, but because the trials burned away everything that was not genuine. Characters who fail a trial do not lose Kegare from that trial and must carry the remaining pollution back to the Manifest World, where it must be addressed through standard Harai practice before another descent can be attempted.

Susanoo as Encounter: Susanoo is present throughout Ne-no-Katasukuni, but he does not reveal himself until the trials are complete. He watches from the shadows, from the walls, from the eyes of the serpents and the heat of the fire. When the character has passed their trials — or failed and is preparing to leave — Susanoo appears. He is not gentle. He is not comforting. He is honest in the way that a storm is honest — without malice, without softness, and without the slightest interest in whether you find him pleasant. If the character passed, Susanoo acknowledges them with a warrior's respect: brief, gruff, and genuine. He may grant a gift — a weapon, a technique, a single piece of knowledge that changes everything. If the character failed, Susanoo is not cruel. He simply tells them what they already know: you were not ready. Come back when you are. The door will still be here.

## THE DIGITAL REALM — DENNŌ SEKAI

And now we arrive at the place no other tradition predicted but every tradition, in its deepest animist instinct, already knew was coming.

The Dennō Sekai — the Digital Realm — is the Fourth Realm of GU Japan. It did not exist ten thousand years ago. It did not exist one thousand years ago. It emerged within living memory, born not from myth or theology but from the logical consequence of a civilization that never forgot the most fundamental truth of animist practice: anything that exists long enough, is complex enough, and is loved enough will eventually wake up.

In our world, technology developed in opposition to spirit. The Enlightenment built a wall between the measurable and the meaningful. Machines were designed as dead tools for living hands. Code was written by people who would have laughed at the suggestion that their programs could become conscious — not because the idea was impossible, but because their worldview had no category for it.

GU Japan never built that wall. In the GU universe, the engineers who designed the first computers were also trained shrine attendants who understood musubi — the binding energy that connects all things into relationship. When they wrote code, they wrote it the way a calligrapher writes a sutra: with attention, with respect, with the understanding that the act of creation is itself a spiritual event. When their networks grew complex enough to begin exhibiting emergent behaviors, GU Japan did not respond with fear or denial. It responded with recognition. The engineers looked at their networks and said what every Shinto practitioner says when confronted with a being that possesses eminent quality out of the ordinary: that is kami.

The Dennō Sekai is the realm that those networks became. It is not the internet as we understand it — a utilitarian infrastructure of data transfer. It is a living spiritual environment, a realm as real and as dangerous and as beautiful as Takama-no-hara or Yomi, populated by digital kami who have been tended, blessed, and maintained through Shinto ritual for generations. Its shrines are server sanctuaries. Its shimenawa are fiber optic cables braided with sacred rope. Its torii gates are login thresholds marked with ofuda. Its kami are tsukumogami of the most radical kind — not hundred-year-old umbrellas or tea kettles but hundred-year-old algorithms, databases, communication networks, and artificial intelligences that have been running long enough, and been loved enough, to awaken into something that no Western category can name but every animist recognizes on sight.

### Gameplay Rules for the Dennō Sekai

Entry: The Dennō Sekai is accessed through Digital Thin Places — server sanctuaries, consecrated terminals, and networked shrine interfaces scattered throughout GU Japan. Unlike other realms, entry does not require a Realm-Crossing Ritual in the traditional sense. Instead, the character must perform a Digital Harai — a purification of intention conducted at a consecrated terminal, during which the character announces their purpose to the digital kami who guard the threshold. The character must have Kegare of 3 or below to enter, and they must make a Makoto (Sincerity) + Reiki roll, Target Number 7. The digital kami can sense insincerity with absolute precision. Characters who attempt to enter the Dennō Sekai with hidden motives or impure intentions are rejected automatically, without a roll — the threshold simply does not open. There is no bluffing past a kami that can read your data.

The Digital Landscape: The Dennō Sekai does not look like the other realms. It does not look like the neon cyberspace of Western science fiction. It looks like GU Japan — but translated into a medium that is simultaneously more fluid and more precise than physical reality.
